Free Hom-groups, Hom-rings and Semisimple modules
Imed Basdouri, Sami Chouaibi, Abdenacer Makhlouf, Esmael Peyghan

Abstract
The purpose of this paper is to introduce and study a Hom-type generalization of rings. We provide their basic properties and and some key constructions. Furthermore, we consider modules over Hom-rings and characterize the category of simple modules and simple Hom-rings. In addition, we extend some classical results and concepts of groups to Hom-groups. We construct free regular Hom-group using Super-Leaf weighted trees and discuss Normal Hom-subgroups, abelianization of regular Hom-group, universality of tensor product of Hom-groups and simple Hom-groups.



Keywords: Hom-group, free Hom-group, Hom-ring, Module over
\alpha
-Hom-ring, tensor product, simple, semisimple.

Introduction

Hom-type objects have been under intensive research in the last decade. The notion of Hom-Lie algebra was
introduced by Hartwig, Larsson, and Silvestrov in
[
6
]
as part of a study of deformations of the Witt and the
Virasoro algebras
[
1
]
,
[
3
]
,
[
4
]
. Hom-associative algebras were introduced in
[
11
]
, where it is shown that the commutator bracket of a Hom-associative algebra gives rise to a Hom-Lie algebra and where a classification of Hom-Lie admissible algebras is established.
The first notion of Hom-group appeared first in
[
2
]
. Then it was introduced as a non-associative analogue of a group in
[
10
]
, where the authors first gave a
new construction of the universal enveloping algebra that is different from the one in
[
13
]
. This new construction
leads to a Hom-Hopf algebra structure on the universal enveloping algebra of a Hom-Lie algebra. One
can associate a Hom-group to any Hom-Lie algebra by considering group-like elements in its universal enveloping
algebra. Recently, M. Hassanzadeh developed representations and a (co)homology theory for Hom-groups in
[
7
]
.
He also proved Lagrange’s theorem for finite Hom-groups in
[
8
]
. In
[
9
]
, Hom-Lie groups and relationship between Hom-Lie groups and
Hom-Lie algebras were explored. The author studied integrable Hom-Lie algebra describe a Hom-Lie group action on a smooth manifold. They define a Hom-exponential (Hexp) map from the Hom-Lie algebra of a Hom-Lie group to the Hom-Lie group and discuss the universality of this Hexp map. We also describe a Hom-Lie group action on a smooth manifold.

The purpose of this paper is to introduce Hom-rings which are twisted versions of the ordinary rings. We discuss
some of their properties and provide construction procedures using ordinary rings. Also, we introduce the
notion of modules on Hom-rings and characterize the category of simple modules and simple Hom-rings. Likewise, some developments of Hom-group theory are provide. In particular free regular Hom-groups are constructed and normal Hom-subgroups explored.
The paper is organized as follows:
In Section 1, we recall some basic definitions and results concerning Hom-groups. In Section 2, we provide a construction of free regular Hom-group based on super-leaf weighted trees. In Section 3, we introduce Normal Hom-subgroups and discuss their properties. In Section 4, we characterize simple Hom-groups and in Section 5 we deal with tensor products.In Section 6, we introduce Hom-type versions of rings
and some related notions. Moreover, we study modules over
Hom-rings in Section 7. Semisimple modules and Hom-rings are characterized in Section 8 and tensor product of modules discussed in the last section.

1
Hom-groups

We review some basics about Hom-groups from
[
10
]
,
[
7
]
and
[
8
]
, and provide some new properties about hom-groups. Also, we recall the notion of Hom-group homomorphism and show a Hom-group structure on the set of all Hom-group homomorphisms between two abelian regular Hom-groups. Finally, we discuss some classes of Hom-subgroups.

1.1
Definitions and Examples

Definition 1.1
.

A Hom-group is a quadruplet
(G,\mu,e,\alpha)
consisting of a set
G
with a distinguished element
e
of
G
, an operation
\mu:G\times G\rightarrow G
and a set map
\alpha:G\rightarrow G
, such that the following axioms are satisfied:

1.

The product map
\mu:G\times G\rightarrow G
and the set map
\alpha:G\rightarrow G
satisfy the Hom-associativity condition

\mu(\alpha(g),\mu(h,k))=\mu(\mu(g,h),\alpha(z)).

(1.1)

For simplicity when there is no confusion we omit the multiplication sign
\mu.

2.

The map
\alpha
is multiplicative, i.e.,
\alpha(\mu(g,h))=\mu(\alpha(g),\alpha(h))
.

3.

The element
e
is called unit and it satisfies the Hom-unitarity condition

\mu(g,e)=\mu(e,g)=\alpha(g)\;\;\quad\alpha(e)=e.

(1.2)

4.

the map
g\longmapsto g^{-1}
is an antimorphism;
\mu(g,h)^{-1}=\mu(h^{-1},g^{-1}).

5.

For any
g\in G
there exists a natural number
k
satisfying the Hom-invertibility condition

\alpha^{k}(\mu(g,g^{-1}))=\alpha^{k}(\mu(g^{-1},g))=e.

(1.3)

The smallest such
k
is called the invertibility index of
g.

If
\alpha
is invertible, condition (
1.3
) can be simplified to the following condition:

For every element
g\in G
, there exists an element
g^{-1}
which
\mu(g,g^{-1})=\mu(g^{-1},g)=e.

A Hom-group, such as
\alpha
is invertible, is called regular Hom-group (
[
8
]
,
[
9
]
). If moreover the product
\mu
is commutative, we say that
G
is an abelian (commutative) Hom-group. In this case, the product
\mu
will be denoted by
+
and the element
e
by
0.
Therefore the multiplicativity axiom will change to
\alpha(g+h)=\alpha(g)+\alpha(h),~\forall g,~h\in G.

Remarks 1.2
.

1.

From the Hom-unitarity and the Hom-associativity conditions, we can show that the multiplicativity of the map
\alpha
is intuitive;

\alpha(\mu(g,h))=\mu(e,\mu(g,h))=\mu(\alpha(e),\mu(g,h))=\mu(\mu(e,g),\alpha(h))=\mu(\alpha(g),\alpha(h)
.

2.

Since we have the antimorphism
g\longmapsto g^{-1}
, therefore by the definition, inverse of any
element
g\in G
is unique although different elements may have different invertibility indexes.

3.

The inverse of the element
e
of a Hom-group is itself because
\alpha(\mu(e,e))=\alpha(e)=e.

4.

For any Hom-group
(G,\mu,e,\alpha)
, we have
\alpha(g^{-1})=\alpha(g)^{-1}
and if the invertibility index of
g
is
k
, then the invertibility index of
\alpha(g)
is
k-1.

Example 1.3
.

Let
(G,\mu,e)
be a group and
\alpha:G\rightarrow G
be a group homomorphism. We define a new product
\mu_{\alpha}:G\times G\rightarrow G
given by

\mu_{\alpha}(g,h)=\alpha(\mu(g,h))=\mu(\alpha(g),\alpha(h)).

Then
(G,\mu_{\alpha},e,\alpha)
is a Hom-group denoted by
G_{\alpha}
. It is said the twist group of
G
.

Example 1.4
.

Let
(G,\mu_{G},e_{G},\alpha_{G})
and
(H,\mu_{H},e_{H},\alpha_{H})
be two Hom-groups. Then
(G\times H,\mu,e,\alpha)
is a Hom-group such as:

•

the identity element
e=(e_{G},e_{H}),

•

the map
\alpha
is defined by
\alpha(g,h)=(\alpha_{G}(g),\alpha_{H}(h)),\;\forall(g,h)\in G\times H,

•

the multiplication is given by
\mu((g_{1},h_{1}),(g_{2},h_{2}))=(\mu_{G}(g_{1},g_{2}),\mu_{H}(h_{1},h_{2})),\;\forall(g_{i},h_{i})\in G\times H,\;i=1,~2.

We have this new example.

Example 1.5
.

Let
(\mathbb{Z},+)
be the additive group of integers. We have
End(\mathbb{Z})\simeq\mathbb{Z}
, i.e., any endomorphism
\alpha\in End(\mathbb{Z})
is completely determined by the element
a(1)\in\mathbb{Z}
and for every
q\in\mathbb{Z}
there exists an endomorphism
\alpha\in End(\mathbb{Z})
such that
\alpha(1)=q
. In other words, the correspondence
\alpha\mapsto\alpha(1)
is an isomorphism between
End(\mathbb{Z})
and
\mathbb{Z}.
Then, for every
q\in\mathbb{Z}
, we can define a Hom-group denoted by
(\mathbb{Z},+_{q},0,\alpha)
such as
+_{q}
is defined by
n+_{q}m=q(n+m),~\forall n,~m\in\mathbb{Z}
and checks the following condition

qn+_{q}(m+_{q}p)=(n+_{q}m)+_{q}qp=q^{2}(n+m+p),\;\forall n,~m,~p\in\mathbb{Z}.

(1.4)

The Hom-group
(\mathbb{Z},+_{q},0,\alpha)
is called the
q
-additive group of integers.

1.2
Some properties

Lemma 1.6
.

Let
(G,\cdot,e,\alpha)
be a Hom-group and
g\in G
. If
g\cdot g=\alpha(g)
and
\alpha^{2}(g)=g
, then
g=e.

Proof
.

Let
h\in G
such that
h\cdot g=e
. Then

\displaystyle\alpha^{2}(g)=e\cdot\alpha(g)=(h\cdot g)\cdot\alpha(g)=\alpha(h)\cdot(g\cdot g)=\alpha(h)\cdot\alpha(g)=\alpha(h\cdot g)=\alpha(e)=e.

From
\alpha^{2}(g)=g
, we can find out that
g=e.

Proposition 1.7
.

Let
(G,\mu,e,\alpha)
be a Hom-group and
g
be an invertible element of index
n
. If
\alpha^{i}(g)h=\alpha^{i}(g)k,~\forall i\geq n
, then we have
\alpha^{2}(h)=\alpha^{2}(k)
.

Proof
.

Using Definition
1.1
, we get

\displaystyle\alpha^{2}(h)=e\alpha(h)=\alpha^{i}(g^{-1}g)\alpha(h)=(\alpha^{i}(g^{-1})\alpha^{i}(g)))\alpha(h)=\alpha^{i+1}(g^{-1})(\alpha^{i}(g)h),\ \ \ \forall i\geq n.

Applying
\alpha^{i}(g)h=\alpha^{i}(g)k
, the above equation reduces to the following:

\displaystyle\alpha^{2}(h)=\alpha^{i+1}(g^{-1})(\alpha^{i}(g)k)=\alpha^{i}(g^{-1}g)\alpha(k)=e\alpha(k)=\alpha^{2}(k).

Corollary 1.8
.

If
(G,\mu,e,\alpha)
is a regular Hom-group,
g
is an invertible element and
gh=gk
, then
h=k
.

Lemma 1.9
.

Let
(G,\mu,e,\alpha)
be a Hom-group,
g
be an invertible element of index
n
and
l
be an invertible element of index
m
. If
(\alpha^{i}(g)h)(k\alpha^{j}(l))=(\alpha^{i}(g)k)(h\alpha^{j}(l)),~\forall i\geq n,~j\geq m
, then
\alpha^{3}(hk)=\alpha^{3}(kh)
.

Proof
.

Using Definition
1.1
, we get

\displaystyle\alpha^{3}(hk)

\displaystyle=\alpha^{3}(h)\alpha^{3}(k)

\displaystyle=\Big(\alpha^{i+2}(g^{-1})\Big(\alpha^{i+1}(g)\alpha(h)\Big)\Big)\Big(\Big(\alpha(k)\alpha^{j+1}(l)\Big)\alpha^{j+2}(l^{-1})\Big)

\displaystyle=\Big(\alpha^{i+2}(g^{-1})\Big(\alpha^{i+1}(g)\alpha(h)\Big)\Big)\alpha\Big((k\alpha^{j}(l))\alpha^{j+1}(l^{-1})\Big)

\displaystyle=\alpha^{i+3}(g^{-1})\Big(\Big(\alpha^{i+1}(g)\alpha(h)\Big)\Big((k\alpha^{j}(l))\alpha^{j+1}(l^{-1})\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\alpha^{i+3}(g^{-1})\Big(\Big((\alpha^{i}(g)h)(k\alpha^{j}(l))\Big)\alpha^{j+2}(l^{-1})\Big)

\displaystyle=\alpha^{i+3}(g^{-1})\Big(\Big((\alpha^{i}(g)k)(h\alpha^{j}(l))\Big)\alpha^{j+2}(l^{-1})\Big)

\displaystyle=\alpha^{i+3}(g^{-1})\Big(\Big(\alpha^{i+1}(g)\alpha(k)\Big)\Big((h\alpha^{j}(l))\alpha^{j+1}(l^{-1})\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\alpha^{i+3}(g^{-1})\Big(\Big(\alpha^{i+1}(g)\alpha(k)\Big)\alpha^{2}(h)\Big)

\displaystyle=\alpha^{i+3}(g^{-1})\Big(\alpha^{i+2}(g)\alpha(kh)\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\alpha^{i+2}(g^{-1}g)\alpha^{2}(kh)

\displaystyle=\alpha^{3}(kh).

Corollary 1.10
.

Let
(G,\mu,e,\alpha)
be a regular Hom-group and
g
,
l
be invertible elements. If
(gh)(kl)=(gk)(hl)
, then
hk=kh
.

Proposition 1.11
.

Let
(G,\mu,e,\alpha)
be a Hom-group,
g
be an invertible element of index
n
and
l
be an invertible element of index
m
.
If
(\alpha^{i}(g^{-1})\alpha^{j}(h^{-1}))(\alpha^{i}(g)\alpha^{j}(h))=e,~\forall i\geq n,~j\geq m
, then
\alpha^{i+5}(g)\alpha^{j+5}(h)=\alpha^{j+5}(h)\alpha^{i+5}(g).

Proof
.

We have
e=\alpha^{i}(g^{-1}g)\alpha^{j}(h^{-1}h)
. Lemma
1.9
gives us
\alpha^{3}(\alpha^{i}(g)\alpha^{j}(h^{-1}))=\alpha^{3}(\alpha^{j}(h^{-1})\alpha^{i}(g)).

By multiplying this last equation on the left by
\alpha^{j++4}(h)
and using the hom-associativity of
\alpha
, we get

\alpha^{i+5}(g)=\alpha^{j+4}(h)\Big(\alpha^{i+3}(g)\alpha^{j+3}(h^{-1})\Big)=\Big(\alpha^{j+3}(h)\alpha^{i+3}(g)\Big)\alpha^{j+3}(h^{-1}).

Multiply this equation on the right by
\alpha^{j++5}(h)
, we find

\alpha^{i+5}(g)\alpha^{j+5}(h)=\alpha^{j+5}(h)\alpha^{i+5}(g).

Corollary 1.12
.

Let
(G,\mu,e,\alpha)
be a regular Hom-group and
g
,
l
be invertible elements of
G
. If
(g^{-1}h^{-1})(gh)=e
, then
gh=hg
.

Proposition 1.13
.

Let
(G,\cdot,e,\alpha)
be a regular abelian Hom-group. For all
g,~h,~k
and
l
in
G
, we have

(g\cdot h)\cdot(k\cdot l)=(g\cdot k)\cdot(h\cdot l).

(1.5)

Proof
.

For all
g,~h,~k
and
l
in
G
, we have

\displaystyle(g\cdot h)\cdot(k\cdot l)

\displaystyle=\alpha(g)\cdot(h\cdot\alpha^{-1}(k\cdot l))=\alpha(g)\cdot(\alpha^{-1}(h\cdot k)\cdot l)=\alpha(g)\cdot(\alpha^{-1}(k\cdot h)\cdot l)

\displaystyle=\alpha(g)\cdot(k\cdot\alpha^{-1}(h\cdot l))=(g\cdot k)\cdot(h\cdot l).

1.3
Hom-groups Homomorphisms

Definition 1.14
.

Let
(G,\mu_{G},e_{G},\alpha_{G})
and
(H,\mu_{H},e_{H},\alpha_{H})
be two Hom-groups.
A homomorphism of Hom-groups is a map
f:G\rightarrow H
such that

f(\mu_{G}(g,h))=\mu_{H}(f(g),f(h)),~\forall g,~h\in G\;\;\text{ and }\;\;f\circ\alpha_{G}=\alpha_{H}\circ f.

It is said to be a weak homomorphism if
f\circ\alpha_{G}=\alpha_{H}\circ f
.

Remark 1.15
.

Let
f:G\rightarrow H
be a homomorphism of Hom-groups such that
f(e_{G})=e_{H}
and
g
be an element in
G
. Then, we have

\displaystyle f(\alpha_{G}(g))=f(\mu_{G}(e_{G},g))=\mu_{H}(f(e_{G}),f(g))=\mu_{H}(e_{H},f(g))=\alpha_{H}(f(g)).

So any homomorphism of Hom-groups
f:(G,\mu_{G},e_{G},\alpha_{G})\rightarrow(H,\mu_{H},e_{H},\alpha_{H})
such that
f(e_{G})=e_{H}
is a weak homomorphism.

Definition 1.16
.

Two Hom-groups
(G,\mu_{G},e_{G},\alpha_{G})
and
(H,\mu_{H},e_{H},\alpha_{H})
are called isomorphic if there exists a bijective
homomorphism of Hom-groups
f:(G,\mu_{G},e_{G},\alpha_{G})\rightarrow(H,\mu_{H},e_{H},\alpha_{H})
.

Proposition 1.17
.

Let
(G,\cdot,e,\alpha)
be a regular Hom-group and define a map
f:G\rightarrow G
by
f(g)=g\cdot g
for each
g\in G
.
Then
G
is an abelian Hom-group if and only if the map
f
is a Hom-group homomorphism.

Proof
.

Suppose that
G
is an abelian Hom-group. Let
g,~h
be arbitrary elements in
G
. Then using Lemma
1.13
, we have

\displaystyle f(g\cdot h)=(g\cdot h)\cdot(g\cdot h)=(g\cdot g)\cdot(h\cdot h)=f(g)\cdot f(h).

Hence
f
is a Hom-group homomorphism from
G
to
G
. Conversely, suppose that
f:G\rightarrow G
by
f(g)=g\cdot g
is a Hom-group homomorphism. Let
g,~h
be arbitrary elements in
G
. We prove that
g\cdot h=h\cdot g
. Since
f
is a Hom-group homomorphism, we have
f(g\cdot h)=f(g)\cdot f(h),
i.e.,
(g\cdot h)\cdot(g\cdot h)=(g\cdot g)\cdot(h\cdot h)
.
By Hom-associativity of the multiplication of
G
, the last equation is equivalent to
\alpha(g)\cdot(h\cdot\alpha^{-1}(g\cdot h))=\alpha(g)\cdot(g\cdot\alpha^{-1}(h\cdot h)).
Multiplying this by
\alpha^{2}(g^{-1})
on the left, we obtain
\alpha^{2}(h)\cdot\alpha(g\cdot h)=\alpha^{2}(g)\cdot\alpha(h\cdot h)
. By Hom-associativity we have
\alpha(h\cdot g)\cdot\alpha^{2}(h)=\alpha(g\cdot h)\cdot\alpha^{2}(h)
. Multiplying this by
\alpha^{3}(h^{-1})
on the right, we find
\alpha^{3}(h\cdot g)=\alpha^{3}(g\cdot h).
Since
\alpha
is bijective, we have
g\cdot h=h\cdot g.
So
G
is abelian.

Proposition 1.18
.

Let
(G,+_{G},e_{G},\alpha_{G})
and
(H,+_{H},e_{H},\alpha_{H})
be abelian regular Hom-groups.

Then
(Hom(G,H),+,0,\alpha)
is an abelian Hom-group, where
Hom(G,H)
is the set of all Hom-group homomorphisms from
G
to
H
,
f+g
is defined by

(f+g)(x)=f(x)+_{H}g(x),\;\;\mbox{for all}\;\;x\in G,

(1.6)

and
\alpha:Hom(G,H)\rightarrow Hom(G,H)
is defined by
\alpha(f)(x)=\alpha_{H}(f(x))
, for all
x\in G.

Proof
.

According to Lemma
1.13
and from the commutativity of
H
, we can prove that
f+g
is again a Hom-group homomorphism. The other properties for
Hom(G,H)
to be an abelian Hom-group are consequences of the fact that
(H,+_{H},e_{H},\alpha_{H})
is an abelian Hom-group.

1.4
Hom-subgroups

Definition 1.19
.

Let
(G,\mu,e,\alpha)
be a Hom-group. A nonempty subset
H
of the Hom-group
G
is a Hom-subgroup of
G
if
(H,\mu,e,\alpha)
is a Hom-group. We use the notation
H\leq G
to indicate that
H
is a Hom-subgroup of
G.

If
H
is a Hom-subgroup, we see that
e
the identity for
G
is also
the identity for
H
. Consequently the following theorem is obvious:

Theorem 1.20
.

A subset
H
of the Hom-group
(G,\cdot,e,\alpha)
is a Hom-subgroup of
G
if and only if

1.

e\in H,

2.

h\in H\Rightarrow h^{-1}\in H,

3.

h_{1},~h_{2}\in H\Rightarrow h_{1}\cdot h_{2}\in H
.

Remarks 1.21
.

1.

From the third item of the previous theorem, one notes that if
H
is a Hom-subgroup of
G
then
\alpha(h)=e\cdot h\in H
for all
h\in H
. Therefore
\alpha(H)\subset H.

2.

Let
(G,\mu,e,\alpha)
be a Hom-group and
H
be a subset of
G
. A Hom-group structure on H is defined by the inclusion map.

Example 1.22
.

Let
G
be a group and
\alpha:G\rightarrow G
be a group homomorphism. If
H
is a subgroup of
G
which
\alpha(H)\subset H
, then
H_{\alpha}
is a Hom-subgroup of
G_{\alpha}.

Example 1.23
.

Let
(G,\mu_{G},e_{G},\alpha_{G})
and
(H,\mu_{H},e_{H},\alpha_{H})
. If
f:G\rightarrow H
is a Hom-group homomorphism such that
f(e_{G})=e_{H}
, then the inverse image of each Hom-subgroup of
H
is a Hom-subgroup of
G.

Definition 1.24
.

[
7
]

Let
(G,\mu,e,\alpha)
be a regular Hom-group. The center
Z(G)
of the Hom-group
(G,\mu,e,\alpha)
is the set of all
g\in G
where
gx=xg
for all
x\in G.

Proposition 1.25
.

[
7
]

(Z(G),\mu,e,\alpha)
is a Hom-subgroup of
(G,\mu,e,\alpha)
.

Lemma 1.26
.

Let
(G,\mu,e,\alpha)
be a regular Hom-group. Then,
\forall g\in Z(G),
we have
\alpha^{k}(g)\in Z(G),~\forall k\in\mathbb{N}.

Proof
.

Let
g\in Z(G)
.

1.

For
k=1
, we have
\alpha(g)x=(eg)x=\alpha(e)(g\alpha^{-1}(x))=e(\alpha^{-1}(x)g)=(e\alpha^{-1}(x))\alpha(g)=x\alpha(g).
Then
\alpha(g)\in Z(G)
.

2.

Suppose that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
we have
\alpha^{k}(g)\in Z(G)
and show that
\alpha^{k+1}(g)
belongs to
Z(G)
;

\alpha^{k+1}(g)x=(e\alpha^{k}(g))x=\alpha(e)(\alpha^{k}(g)\alpha^{-1}(x))=e(\alpha^{-1}(x)\alpha^{k}(g))=(e\alpha^{-1}(x))\alpha^{k+1}(g)=x\alpha^{k+1}(g).

Let
(G,\mu,e)
be a group and
(G,\mu_{\alpha},e,\alpha)
the associated twist group of
(G,\mu,e)
. Consider the set

Z_{\alpha}(G)=\{g\in G/\alpha(gx)=\alpha(xg),~\;\forall x\in G\}.

Then, we have the following proposition

Proposition 1.27
.

If
Z(G)
is the center of the group
(G,\mu,e)
, so we have

1.

Z(G)\subset\alpha^{-1}(\alpha(Z(G)))\subset Z_{\alpha}(G).

2.

Z(G)=Z_{\alpha}(G)
if
\alpha
is injective.

3.

If
\alpha(Z(G))\subset Z(G)
and
\alpha
is injective, then
Z_{\alpha}(G)
is a Hom-subgroup of
(G,\mu_{\alpha},e,\alpha).

Proof
.

1.

Let
g\in\alpha^{-1}(\alpha(Z(G)))
. Then, it exists
h\in Z(G)
such as
\alpha(g)=\alpha(h).
Let us show that
g
is an element in
Z_{\alpha}(G)
, i.e.,
\alpha(gx)=\alpha(xg),~\;\forall x\in G.

\displaystyle\alpha(gx)=\alpha(g)\alpha(x)=\alpha(h)\alpha(x)=\alpha(hx)=\alpha(xh)=\alpha(x)\alpha(h)=\alpha(x)\alpha(g)=\alpha(xg).

This shows that
g\in Z_{\alpha}(G).

2.

Let
g\in Z_{\alpha}(G)
. So,
\alpha(gx)=\alpha(xg)
for all
x\in G
. Since
\alpha
is injective, then
gx=xg,~\forall x\in G
. Therefore,
Z_{\alpha}(G)\subset Z(G).

3.

According to Example
1.22
and the previous property, we can show that
Z_{\alpha}(G)
is a Hom-subgroup of
(G,\mu_{\alpha},e,\alpha)
.

2
Free regular Hom-group

2.1
Free regular Hom-group with
1-
generator

A planar tree is an oriented graph drawn on a plane with only one root. It is called binary when any vertex is trivalent, i.e.,
one root and two leaves. Usually we draw the root at the bottom of the tree and the leaves are drawn at
the top of it:

Root
Leaves

For any natural number
n\geq 1
, let
T_{n}
denote the set of planar binary trees with
n
leaves and one root. The first
T_{n}
are depicted below.

An element in
T_{n}
will be called an
n
-tree. When necessary we label the leaves of an
n
-tree by

1,~2,~3,~\cdot\cdot\cdot,~n
from left to right.

Let
\psi\in T_{n}
and
\varphi\in T_{m}
be a pair of trees, the
(n+m)
-tree
\psi\vee\varphi
, called the grafting of
\psi
and
\varphi
, is
obtained by joining the roots of
\psi
and
\varphi
to create a new root. For instance,

Remark 2.1
.

Note that grafting is a nonassociative and non-commutative operation. For any tree
\varphi\in T_{n}
, there are unique integers
p
and
q
with
p+q=n
and trees
\varphi_{1}\in T_{p}
and
\varphi_{2}\in T_{q}
such that
\varphi=\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2}
. The tree
\varphi_{1}
is called the left subtree (L-subtree) of
\varphi
and
\varphi_{2}
is the right subtree (R-subtree) of
\varphi
. So any tree
\varphi
will be represented as follows

\varphi=\hbox to90.7pt{\vbox to32.27pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 33.01851pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

Definition 2.2
.

Let
\varphi=\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2}
be a tree. Two successive leaves of
\varphi
are called

1.

left (resp. right) attached leaves (LAL resp. RAL) if they have the same node in the left (resp. right) subtree of
\varphi
:

\overbrace{}^{LAL}

\overbrace{}^{RAL}

2.

left (resp. right) disjoint leaves (LDL resp. RDL) if they don’t have the same node in the left (resp. right) subtree of
\varphi
:

\overbrace{}^{LDL}

\overbrace{}^{RDL}

3.

disjoint leaves (DL) if the first leaf is the last leaf of the left subtree of
\varphi
and the other is the first leaf of the right subtree of
\varphi
:

\hbox to51.62pt{\vbox to61.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-11.58179pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

Definition 2.3
.

A bicolored Leaf
n
-tree is a
n
-tree such that each leaf is colored with black (b) or white (w). The set of bicolored Leaf
n
-tree will be denoted by
D_{n}
.

.

Definition 2.5
.

A Super-Leaf weighted
n
-tree is a pair
(\varphi,a)
where:

•

\varphi
is an element of
D_{n}
,

•

a
is an
n-
tuplet
(a_{1},a_{2},\cdot\cdot\cdot,a_{n})
such that, for all
1\leq i\leq n,~a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}.

We call the bicolored Leaf tree
\varphi
the underlying tree of the Super-Leaf weighted
n
-tree
(\varphi,a)
. For all
1\leq i\leq n
, the integer
a_{i}
is said to be the weight of the leaf
i
.

We will indeed barely use the notation
(\varphi,a)
at all, and find more convenient to picture a Super-leaf weighted
n
-tree
(\varphi,a_{1},a_{2},\cdot\cdot\cdot,a_{n})
by drawing the tree
\varphi
and putting the weight
a_{i}
next to each leaf. For example, here are a Super-leaf weighted
2
-tree and a Super-leaf weighted
3
-tree:

2
3

-2
1
5

The grafting operation extends to leaf weighted
n
-trees. For example:

For
n\geq 1
, we let
B_{n}
denote the set of all Super-Leaf weighted
n
-trees. Let
B
denote the union over
n\in\mathbb{N}
of the sets
B_{n}
together with an element that we call the unit and denote by
\mathbf{1}
. Note that the element
\mathbf{1}
is different from the Super-Leaf weighted
1
-tree

We then consider the free vector space
\mathbb{T}
generated by the set
B
.

We define on
\mathbb{T}
two natural linear maps:

1.

\alpha:\mathbb{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{T}
sending
\mathbf{1}
to
\mathbf{1}
and sending a Super-Leaf weighted
n
-tree
(\varphi,a_{1},a_{2},\cdot\cdot\cdot,a_{n})
to the Super-Leaf weighted
n
-trees
(\varphi,a_{1}+1,a_{2}+2,\cdot\cdot\cdot,a_{n}+1).

2.

a product
\vee
that, for any pair of Super-Leaf weighted trees, is just the grafting of these trees and such
that for any Super-Leaf weighted
=\varphi=(\varphi,a)

\mathbf{1}\vee\varphi=\varphi\vee\mathbf{1}=\alpha(\varphi)

(2.1)

Remark 2.6
.

\alpha(\mathbf{1})=\mathbf{1}

a\in\mathbb{Z}

The proof of the following lemma is obvious.

Lemma 2.7
.

The map
\alpha
defined above is a morphism for the grafting of trees, that is

\alpha(\varphi\vee\psi)=\alpha(\varphi)\vee\alpha(\psi),\;\mbox{for any}\;\varphi,\psi\in\mathbb{T}.

In addition,
\alpha
is bijective and
\alpha^{-1}
is defined, for all
(\varphi,a_{1},a_{2},\cdot\cdot\cdot,a_{n})\in\mathbb{T}
, by

\alpha^{-1}(\varphi,a_{1},a_{2},\cdot\cdot\cdot,a_{n})=(\varphi,a_{1}-1,a_{2}-1,\cdot\cdot\cdot,a_{n}-1).

Definition 2.8
.

Two leaves of a Super-Leaf weighted
n
-tree are said inverse type leaves if one is colored by b then the other is colored by w.

We now define a reduction process which allows one to obtain a reduced Super-Leaf weighted
\Omega
-type
n
-trees from an arbitrary Leaf weighted
\Omega
-type
n
-tree.

Definition 2.9
.

Let
\varphi
be a Super-Leaf weighted
n
-tree. An elementary reduction of
\varphi
is defined as follows

1.

If the leaf
i
and the leaf
i+1
are two left attached and inverse type leaves of the same weight, then an elementary reduction of
\varphi
is obtained by replacing the leaf
i
and the leaf
i+1
by
\mathbf{1}.

2.

If the leaf
i
and the leaf
i+1
are two right attached and inverse type leaves of the same weight, then an elementary reduction of
\varphi
is obtained by replacing the leaf
i
and the leaf
i+1
by
\mathbf{1}.

3.

If the leaf
i
and the leaf
i+1
are two left disjoint and inverse type leaves of weights respectively
b
and
b+1
, then an elementary reduction of
\varphi
is obtained by replacing the leaf
i
and the leaf
i+1
by
\mathbf{1}.

4.

If the leaf
i
and the leaf
i+1
are two right disjoint and inverse type leaves of weights respectively
b+1
and
b
, then an elementary reduction of
\varphi
is obtained by replacing the leaf
i
and the leaf
i+1
by
\mathbf{1}.

5.

If the leaf
i
and the leaf
i+1
are two DL1 and inverse type leaves of the same weight, then an elementary reduction of
\varphi
is obtained by replacing the leaf
i
and the leaf
i+1
by
\mathbf{1}.

6.

If the leaf
i
and the leaf
i+1
are two DL2 and inverse type leaves of weights respectively
b
and
b+1
, then an elementary reduction of
\varphi
is obtained by replacing the leaf
i
and the leaf
i+1
by
\mathbf{1}.

7.

If the leaf
i
and the leaf
i+1
are two DL3 and inverse type leaves of weights respectively
b+1
and
b
, then an elementary reduction of
\varphi
is obtained by replacing the leaf
i
and the leaf
i+1
by
\mathbf{1}.

8.

If the leaf
i
and the leaf
i+1
are two DL4 and inverse type leaves of of the same weight, then an elementary reduction of
\varphi
is obtained by replacing the leaf
i
and the leaf
i+1
by
\mathbf{1}.

Definition 2.11
.

Let
\varphi
be a Super-Leaf weighted tree. A reduction of
\varphi
(or a reduction process starting at
\varphi
) consists of consequent applications of elementary reductions starting at
\varphi
and ending at a reduced Super-Leaf weighted tree:

\displaystyle\varphi\rightarrow\varphi_{1}\rightarrow\varphi_{2}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\varphi_{n}.

The Super-Leaf weighted tree
\varphi_{n}
is termed the reduced Super-Leaf weighted tree of
\varphi
and it is denoted
\overline{\varphi}.

.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

In general, there are different possible reductions of a Super-Leaf weighted tree
\varphi
. Nevertheless, it turns out that all possible reductions of
\varphi
end up with the same reduced Super-Leaf weighted tree. To see this we need the following lemma.

Lemma 2.13
.

For any two elementary reductions
\varphi\rightarrow\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi\rightarrow\varphi_{2}
of a Super-Leaf weighted tree
\varphi
, there exist elementary reductions
\varphi_{1}\rightarrow\varphi_{0}
and
\varphi_{2}\rightarrow\varphi_{0}.

Proof
.

Let
\varphi\xrightarrow{\lambda_{1}}\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi\xrightarrow{\lambda_{2}}\varphi_{2}
be two elementary reductions of a Super-Leaf weighted tree
\varphi
. There are two possible ways to carry out the reductions
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}.

Case1: Disjoint reductions

In this case
\varphi=\psi_{1}\vee\psi_{2}
and
\lambda_{1}(\varphi)=\overline{\psi_{1}}\vee\psi_{2}
,
\lambda_{2}(\varphi)=\psi_{1}\vee\overline{\psi_{2}}
. Then

\displaystyle\varphi\xrightarrow{\lambda_{1}}\overline{\psi_{1}}\vee\psi_{2}\xrightarrow{\lambda_{2}}\overline{\psi_{1}}\vee\overline{\psi_{2}}

\displaystyle\varphi\xrightarrow{\lambda_{2}}\psi_{1}\vee\overline{\psi_{2}}\xrightarrow{\lambda_{1}}\overline{\psi_{1}}\vee\overline{\psi_{2}}.

Hence the lemma holds.

Case2: Overlapping reductions

In this case
\varphi
can take the following form

\varphi=(\psi_{1}\vee\hbox to25.61pt{\vbox to24.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.97595pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

Proposition 2.14
.

Let
\varphi
be a Super-Leaf weighted tree. Then any two reductions of
\varphi:

\displaystyle\varphi\rightarrow\varphi^{\prime}_{1}\rightarrow\varphi^{\prime}_{2}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\varphi^{\prime}_{n}

\displaystyle\varphi\rightarrow\varphi"_{1}\rightarrow\varphi"_{2}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\varphi"_{m}

result in the same reduced Super-Leaf weighted tree of
\varphi,
i. e,
\varphi^{\prime}_{n}=\varphi"_{n}.

Proof
.

By induction on
\varphi.
If
\varphi=\mathbf{1}
or
\varphi
is a Super-Leaf weighted
1
-tree, then
\varphi
is reduced and there is nothing to prove.

Let
\varphi\in B_{r},~r\geq 1
and

\displaystyle\varphi\rightarrow\varphi^{\prime}_{1}\rightarrow\varphi^{\prime}_{2}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\varphi^{\prime}_{n}

\displaystyle\varphi\rightarrow\varphi"_{1}\rightarrow\varphi"_{2}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\varphi"_{m}

be two reductions of
\varphi.
Then by Lemma
2.13
, there are elementary reductions
\varphi^{\prime}_{1}\rightarrow\varphi_{0}
and
\varphi"_{1}\rightarrow\varphi_{0}.
Consider a reduction process for
\varphi_{0}:

\displaystyle\varphi_{0}\rightarrow\varphi_{1}\rightarrow\varphi_{2}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\varphi_{k}.

By induction all reduced Super-Leaf weighted trees of
\varphi^{\prime}_{1}
are equal to each other, as well as all reduced Super-Leaf weighted trees of
\varphi"_{1}
. Since
\varphi_{k}
is a reduced Super-Leaf weighted tree of both
\varphi^{\prime}_{1}
and
\varphi"_{1}
, then
\varphi^{\prime}_{n}=\varphi_{k}=\varphi"_{m}
as desired. This proves the proposition.

Let
G
be the set of all reduced Super-Leaf weighted trees.

We now intend to define inverse of a Super-Leaf weighted
n
-trees to hold the structure of Hom-group on
G
. We first define Mirror inverse type of a Super-Leaf weighted
n
-tree(
n\geq 2
).

Definition 2.15
.

Mirror inverse type of a Super-Leaf weighted
n
-trees(
n\geq 2
)
(\varphi,V)
is another Super-Leaf weighted
n
-trees
M(\varphi)
with left and right children of all nodes interchanged and inversing the type of every leaf. We note
\mathbf{MRinv}
the application that sends each Super-Leaf weighted
n
-trees to its mirror inverse type.

Examples 2.16
.

Now, we define the inverse of Super-Leaf weighted
n
-trees by the map
\digamma:\mathbb{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{T}
sending
\mathbf{1}
to
\mathbf{1}
, a Super-Leaf weighted
\Omega
-type
1
-tree
a
to
a
, a Super-Leaf weighted
\Omega
-type
1
-tree
a
to
a
and a super-Leaf weighted
n
-tree (
n\geq 2
)
(\varphi,a)
to it’s Mirror inverse.

According to the definition
2.9
, The proof of the following lemma is trivial:

Lemma 2.17
.

For all
\varphi
and
\psi
in
\mathbb{T}
, we have

\digamma(\varphi\vee\psi)=\digamma(\psi)\vee\digamma(\varphi).

Theorem 2.18
.

Let
\widetilde{\alpha}
be the bijevtive map defined on
G
by, for all
\varphi\in\mathbb{T},~\widetilde{\alpha}(\overline{\varphi})=\overline{\alpha(\varphi)}
and
\widetilde{\alpha}^{-1}(\overline{\varphi})=\overline{\alpha^{-1}(\varphi)}
. If we define a multiplication ”
\cdot
” on
G
by

\overline{\varphi}\cdot\overline{\psi}=\overline{\varphi\vee\psi},\;\;\forall\overline{\varphi},~\overline{\psi}\in G,

we have that
(G,\cdot,\mathbf{1},\widetilde{\alpha})
is a regular Hom-group.

G
will be said the free regular Hom-group with
1
-generator.

Proof
.

1.

The map
\widetilde{\alpha}
is multiplicative:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\alpha}(\overline{\varphi}\cdot\overline{\psi})=\widetilde{\alpha}(\overline{\varphi\vee\psi})=\overline{\alpha(\varphi\vee\psi)}=\overline{\alpha(\varphi)\vee\alpha(\psi)}=\overline{\alpha(\varphi)}\cdot\overline{\alpha(\psi)}=\widetilde{\alpha}(\overline{\varphi})\cdot\widetilde{\alpha}(\overline{\psi})

2.

The multiplication ”
\cdot
” is Hom-associative:

\widetilde{\alpha}(\overline{\varphi})\cdot(\overline{\psi}\cdot\overline{\phi})=(\overline{\varphi}\cdot\overline{\psi})\cdot\widetilde{\alpha}(\overline{\phi}),

for any
\overline{\varphi},~\overline{\psi},~\overline{\phi}\in G
. To show this, it enough to prove that

\overline{\overline{\alpha(\varphi)}\vee(\overline{\psi\vee\phi})}=\overline{(\overline{\varphi\vee\psi})\vee\overline{\alpha(\phi)}},

for given
\varphi,~\psi,~\phi\in\mathbb{T}
. We will observe, that each of the reduced Super-Leaf weighted trees
\overline{\overline{\alpha(\varphi)}\vee(\overline{\psi\vee\phi})},

\overline{(\overline{\varphi\vee\psi})\vee\overline{\alpha(\phi)}}
can be obtained from a same Super-Leaf weighted tree by a sequence of elementary reductions. We will show this by induction on
\psi
.

If
\psi=\mathbf{1}
, there is nothing to prove.

If
\psi=\hbox to11.95pt{\vbox to27.02pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.55324pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})\vee\alpha(\phi).

If
\psi\in B_{n}
, then
\psi
can take the following form
\psi=\psi_{1}\vee\psi_{2}
, where
\psi_{1}\in B_{p}
and
\psi_{2}\in B_{q},
such that
p+q=n.
By Proposition
2.14
, we have
\overline{\psi}=\overline{\psi_{1}\vee\psi_{2}}=\overline{\psi_{1}}\cdot\overline{\psi_{2}}
. Then,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\alpha}(\overline{\varphi})\cdot(\overline{\psi}\cdot\overline{\phi})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\alpha}(\overline{\varphi})\cdot((\overline{\psi_{1}}\cdot\overline{\psi_{2}})\cdot\overline{\phi})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\alpha}(\overline{\varphi})\cdot(\widetilde{\alpha}(\overline{\psi_{1}})\cdot(\overline{\psi_{2}}\cdot\widetilde{\alpha}^{-1}(\overline{\phi}))

\displaystyle=(\overline{\varphi}\cdot\widetilde{\alpha}(\overline{\psi_{1}}))\cdot(\widetilde{\alpha}(\overline{\psi_{2}})\cdot\overline{\phi}),

and

\displaystyle(\overline{\varphi}\cdot\overline{\psi})\cdot\widetilde{\alpha}(\overline{\phi})

\displaystyle=(\overline{\varphi}\cdot(\overline{\psi_{1}}\cdot(\overline{\psi_{2}}))\cdot\widetilde{\alpha}(\overline{\phi})

\displaystyle=((\widetilde{\alpha}^{-1}(\overline{\varphi})\cdot\overline{\psi_{1}})\cdot\widetilde{\alpha}(\overline{\psi_{2}}))\cdot\widetilde{\alpha}(\overline{\phi})

\displaystyle=(\overline{\varphi}\cdot\widetilde{\alpha}(\overline{\psi_{1}}))\cdot(\widetilde{\alpha}(\overline{\psi_{2}})\cdot\overline{\phi}).

Hence by Proposition
2.14
, we have
\widetilde{\alpha}(\overline{\varphi})\cdot(\overline{\psi}\cdot\overline{\phi})=(\overline{\varphi}\cdot\overline{\psi})\cdot\widetilde{\alpha}(\overline{\phi}).

3.

The inverse for every
\overline{\varphi}\in G
, is defined by
\overline{\digamma(\varphi)}:

\displaystyle\overline{\varphi}\cdot\overline{\digamma(\varphi)}=\overline{\varphi\vee\digamma(\varphi)}=\overline{\mathbf{1}}=\mathbf{1}.

2.2
Free regular Hom-group

Let
X
be a nonempty set. We define
\mathbb{T}^{X}
by
\mathbb{T}^{X}=\mathbf{1\oplus}\bigoplus_{n\geq 1}B_{n}\otimes X
. Elements of
B_{n}\otimes X
shall be pictured for every
\varphi\in B_{n},~x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\in X
, by inserting, for all
i=1,\cdots,n

the element
x_{i}
at the top of the leaf with label
x_{i}
:

So, the map
\alpha
sends each element
\varphi\otimes(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})
of
B_{n}\otimes X
to
\alpha(\varphi)\otimes(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}).

Also the reduction process and the map
\mathbf{MRinv}
have natural extensions to
\mathbb{T}^{X}
. They are defined as follows.

Definition 2.19
.

The reduction of an element of
\mathbb{T}^{X}
is defined by the same process as in Definition
2.9
but for two leaves labeled by the same
x\in X.

Definition 2.21
.

Let
\varphi\otimes(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})
be an element of
B_{n}\otimes X
. Then
\mathbf{MRinv}(\varphi\otimes(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}))=\mathbf{Minv}(\varphi)\otimes(x_{n},\cdots,x_{1}).

Definition 2.23
.

The set of all reduced elements of
B_{n}\otimes X
is the free regular Hom-group.

3
Normal Hom-subgroups

We introduce in this section Normal Ho-subgroups and discuss their properties.

3.1
Definitions and Examples

Definition 3.1
.

Let
(G,\mu_{G},e,\alpha)
be a regular Hom-group. A Hom-subgroup
H
of
G
is called a normal Hom-subgroup of
G
if

\forall g\in G,\;\;gH=Hg.

(3.1)

We indicate that
H
is a normal Hom-subgroup of
G
with the notation
H\unrhd G.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
H
be a Hom-subgroup of a regular Hom-group
(G,\mu,e,\alpha)
.
H
is a normal Hom-subgroup if and only if

\forall g\in G,\;\forall h\in H,\;\;(gh)\alpha(g^{-1})\in H.

(3.2)

Proof
.

Let
H
be a normal Hom-subgroup of a regular Hom-group
(G,\mu_{G},e,\alpha).
Then, for all
g\in G
and
h\in H
, we have
gh=hg
. Multiply this equation on the right by
\alpha(g^{-1})
, we obtain

\displaystyle(gh)\alpha(g^{-1})=(hg)\alpha(g^{-1})=\alpha(h)(gg^{-1})=\alpha^{2}(h)\in H.

So, for all
g\in G
and
h\in H
, we have
(gh)\alpha(g^{-1})=\alpha(g)(hg^{-1})\in H
. Conversely, let
g\in G
and
h\in H
such that
(gh)\alpha(g^{-1})\in H
. There exists
h^{\prime}\in H
such that
(gh)\alpha(g^{-1})=\alpha^{2}(h^{\prime})
. By
multiplying this last equality on the right by
\alpha^{2}(g)
, we find

\Big((gh)\alpha(g^{-1})\Big)\alpha^{2}(g)=\alpha^{2}(h^{\prime})\alpha^{2}(g),

which gives us

\alpha(gh)\alpha(g^{-1}g)=\alpha^{2}(h^{\prime}g),

or

\alpha^{2}(gh)=\alpha^{2}(h^{\prime}g).

So, the invrtibility of the map
\alpha
gives that
gh=h^{\prime}g
. Therefore, we have
gH=Hg
.

The following lemmas give examples of normal subgroups.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
(G,\mu,e,\alpha)
be a regular Hom-group and
Z(G)=\{g\in G:gx=xg\;\forall x\in G\}
be the center of the Hom-group
G
. Then
Z(G)
is a normal Hom-subgroup.

Proof
.

Let
g
be an element in
G
and
h\in Z(G).
Then
(gh)\alpha(g^{-1})=(hg)\alpha(g^{-1})=\alpha(h)(gg^{-1})=\alpha(h)e=\alpha^{2}(h).
According to Lemma
1.26
, one can show that
(gh)\alpha(g^{-1})\in Z(G).

Definition 3.4
.

Let
(G,\cdot,e,\alpha)
be a regular Hom-group and
H\leq G.
The centralizer
C_{G}(H)
and the normalizer
N_{G}(H)
of the Hom-subgroup
H
are defined respectively as follows

•

C_{G}(H)=\{g\in G:\forall h\in H,~(gh)\alpha(g^{-1})=\alpha^{2}(h)\},

•

N_{G}(H)=\{g\in G:\forall h\in H,~(gh)\alpha(g^{-1})\in H\}.

Lemma 3.5
.

C_{G}(H)
and
N_{G}(H)
are two Hom-subgroups of
G
and
C_{G}(H)\unrhd N_{G}(H).

Proof
.

Let us show first that
C_{G}(H)\leq G;

•

For all
h\in H
, we have
(e\cdot h)\alpha(e)=\alpha(h)e=\alpha^{2}(h)
. Then
e\in C_{G}(H).

•

Let
g,g^{\prime}\in C_{G}(H).
We want to prove that
gg^{\prime}\in C_{G}(H);

\displaystyle((gg^{\prime})h)\alpha(g^{\prime-1}g^{-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\alpha(g)(g^{\prime}\alpha^{-1}(h)))\alpha(g^{\prime-1}g^{-1})=\alpha^{2}(g)((g^{\prime}\alpha^{-1}(h))(g^{\prime-1}g^{-1}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha^{2}(g)(((\alpha^{-1}(g^{\prime})\alpha^{-2}(h))g^{\prime-1})\alpha(g^{-1}))=\alpha^{2}(g)(\alpha^{-1}((g^{\prime}\alpha^{-1}(h))\alpha(g^{\prime-1}))\alpha(g^{-1}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha^{2}(g)(h\alpha(g^{-1}))=(\alpha(g)h)\alpha^{2}(g^{-1})=\alpha((g\alpha^{-1}(h))\alpha(g^{-1}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha^{2}(h).

Consequently, we have
gg^{\prime}\in C_{G}(H).

•

Let
g\in C_{G}(H).
We obtain

\displaystyle(g^{-1}h)\alpha(g)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(g^{-1}(\alpha^{-2}(gh)\alpha^{-1}(g^{-1})))\alpha(g)=(g^{-1}(\alpha^{-1}(g)\alpha^{-2}(hg^{-1})))\alpha(g)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\alpha^{-1}(g^{-1}g)\alpha^{-1}(hg^{-1}))\alpha(g)=(hg^{-1})\alpha(g)=\alpha(h)(g^{-1}g)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha^{2}(h).

So, for all
g\in C_{G}(H),
we have
g^{-1}\in C_{G}(H)
. Therefore,
C_{G}(H)\leq G
. Similarly, we can show that
N_{G}(H)\leq G.

Now, we prove that
C_{G}(H)\unrhd N_{G}(H).
Let
n\in N_{G}(H)
and
c\in C_{G}(H),
for all
h\in H
, we have

\displaystyle[((nc)\alpha(n^{-1}))h]\alpha[\alpha(n)(c^{-1}n^{-1}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[\alpha(nc)(\alpha(n^{-1})\alpha^{-1}(h))]\alpha[\alpha(n)(c^{-1}n^{-1})]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha^{2}(nc)[(\alpha(n^{-1})\alpha^{-1}(h))(\alpha(n)(c^{-1}n^{-1}))]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha^{2}(nc)[((n^{-1}\alpha^{-2}(h))\alpha(n))\alpha(c^{-1}n^{-1})]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha^{2}(nc)(h^{\prime}\alpha(c^{-1}n^{-1})\;\;\mbox{where}\;\;h^{\prime}=(n^{-1}\alpha^{-2}(h))\alpha(n)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha^{2}(nc)((\alpha^{-1}(h^{\prime})\alpha(c^{-1}))\alpha^{2}(n^{-1}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\alpha(nc)(\alpha^{-1}(h^{\prime})\alpha(c^{-1})))\alpha^{3}(n^{-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\alpha^{2}(n)(\alpha(c)(\alpha^{-2}(h^{\prime})c^{-1})))\alpha^{3}(n^{-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\alpha^{2}(n)h^{\prime})\alpha^{3}(n^{-1}).

So
(nc)\alpha(n^{-1})
is in
C_{G}(H)
. Indeed

\displaystyle(\alpha^{2}(n)h^{\prime})\alpha^{3}(n^{-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\alpha^{2}(n)((n^{-1}\alpha^{-2}(h))\alpha(n)))\alpha^{3}(n^{-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle((\alpha(n)(n^{-1}\alpha^{-2}(h)))\alpha^{2}(n))\alpha^{3}(n^{-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(h\alpha^{2}(n))\alpha^{3}(n^{-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha^{2}(h).

Lemma 3.6
.

Every Hom-subgroup of an abelian Hom-group is normal.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
(G\times H,\cdot,e,\alpha)
be the Hom-group defined in (
1.4
). Then
(G\times\{e_{H}\},\cdot,e,\alpha)
is a normal Hom-subgroup of
(G\times H,\cdot,e,\alpha)

Proof
.

For all
g_{1},~g_{2}\in G
and
h\in H
, we have

\displaystyle((g_{1},h)(g_{2},e_{H}))\alpha(g_{1}^{-1},h^{-1})=((g_{1}g_{2})\alpha_{G}(g_{1}^{-1}),\alpha_{H}(hh^{-1}))=((g_{1}g_{2})\alpha_{G}(g_{1}^{-1}),e_{H}).

So, we have the lemma.

Proposition 3.8
.

Let
f:G\rightarrow H
be a Hom-group homomorphism between the Hom-group
(G,\cdot,e_{G},\alpha_{G})
and the Hom-group
(H,\cdot,e_{H},\alpha_{H})
.
If
f(e_{G})=e_{H}
, then
Ker(f)=\{g\in G/f(g)=e_{H}\}
is a normal Hom-subgroup of
G.

Proof
.

According to
[
7
]
,
Ker(f)
is a Hom-subgroup of
G.
Let now
g\in G
and
h\in Ker(f)
. We have

\displaystyle f((gh)\alpha_{G}(g^{-1}))=(f(g)f(h))f(\alpha_{G}(g^{-1}))=(f(g)e_{H})\alpha_{H}(f(g^{-1}))=\alpha_{H}(f(g)f(g^{-1}))=\alpha_{H}(e_{H})=e_{H}.

Therefore
Ker(f)\unrhd G.

A consequence of this proposition is the following corollary

Corollary 3.9
.

Let
(G,\cdot,e,\alpha)
be a Hom-group. Then
Ker(\alpha)\unrhd G.

Proposition 3.10
.

Let
f:G\rightarrow H
be a Hom-group homomorphism between the Hom-group
(G,\cdot,e_{G},\alpha_{G})
and the Hom-group
(H,\cdot,e_{H},\alpha_{H})
such that
f(e_{G})=e_{H}
.

1.

If
N\unrhd G
, then
f(N)\unrhd f(G).

2.

If
M\unrhd f(G)
, then
f^{-1}(M)\unrhd G.

Proof
.

1.

Let
h\in f(G)
and
n\in N
. If
g\in G
such that
f(g)=h,
we have

(hf(n))\alpha_{H}(h^{-1})=(f(g)f(n))\alpha_{H}(f(g^{-1}))=f(gn)f(\alpha_{G}(g^{-1}))=f((gn)\alpha_{G}(g^{-1}))\in f(N),

since
N\unrhd G
. So
f(N)\unrhd f(G).

2.

Let
g\in G
and
n\in f^{-1}(M)
, we have

f((gn)\alpha_{G}(g^{-1}))=(f(g)f(n))f(\alpha_{G}(g^{-1}))=(f(g)f(n))\alpha_{H}(f(g^{-1}))\in M,

since
M\unrhd f(G)
, proving the proposition.

3.2
Quotient Hom-group

Definition 3.11
.

If
H
is a Hom-subgroup of a Hom-group
(G,\cdot,e,\alpha)
and
g\in G
then

gH=\{gh/h\in H\},

is a left coset of
H
.

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
H
be a Hom-subgroup of a regular Hom-group
(G,\cdot,e,\alpha)
and
g,g^{\prime}\in G
. Suppose
\alpha(H)=H.
Then
gH=g^{\prime}H
if and only if
g^{-1}g^{\prime}\in H.

Proof
.

Suppose
gH=g^{\prime}H
. Then
\alpha(g^{\prime})\in g^{\prime}H
and so
\alpha(g^{\prime})\in gH
. Thus
\alpha(g^{\prime})=gh
for some
h\in H
and we see that
g^{-1}g^{\prime}=\alpha^{-1}(h)\in H
, since
\alpha(H)=H.
Conversely, suppose
g^{-1}g^{\prime}\in H.
Then
g^{-1}g^{\prime}=h
, for some
h\in H
. Thus
\alpha^{2}(g^{\prime})=\alpha(g)h
and consequently
\alpha^{2}(g^{\prime})H=(\alpha(g)h)\alpha(H)=\alpha^{2}(g)(hH)
. Observe that
hH=H
because
h\in H
.
Therefore
gH=g^{\prime}H
, since
\alpha^{2}(g)H=\alpha^{2}(gh)
.

Theorem 3.13
.

Let
H
be a Hom-subgroup of a regular Hom-group
(G,\cdot,e,\alpha)
and consider the set
G/H=\{gH/g\in G\}
.
If
H\unrhd G
, then
(G/H,\cdot,eH,\widetilde{\alpha})
is a Hom-group, where

•

\widetilde{\alpha}:G/H\rightarrow G/H
is defined by
\widetilde{\alpha}(gH)=\alpha(g)H
,

•

g_{1}H\cdot g_{2}H=(g_{1}g_{2})H.

Proof
.

1.

For all
g_{1},~g_{2}\in G
, we have

\displaystyle(g_{1}H)(g_{2}H)

\displaystyle=\alpha(g_{1})(H\alpha^{-1}(g_{2}H))=\alpha(g_{1})(\alpha^{-1}(Hg_{2})H)=\alpha(g_{1})(\alpha^{-1}(g_{2}H)H)

\displaystyle=\alpha(g_{1})(g_{2}\alpha^{-1}(HH))=(g_{1}g_{2})(HH)=(g_{1}g_{2})H.

2.

Since the multiplication of
G
is Hom-associative, then the multiplication of
G/H
is Hom-associative.

3.

eH
is the identity in
G/H
.

4.

Then inverse of
gH
is
g^{-1}H.

3.3
Commutator Hom-subgroup

Definition 3.14
.

Let
(G,\mu,e,\alpha)
be a regular Hom-group. The Commutator Hom-subgroup
N=[G,G]
of
G
is the set of the elements

[g,h]=(g^{-1}h^{-1})(gh),\;\;\forall g,~h\in G.

(3.3)

Proposition 3.15
.

The Commutator subgroup
N=[G,G]
of a regular Hom-group
(G,\mu,e,\alpha)
is a normal Hom-subgroup.

Proof
.

Let
s,~g,~h\in G
. Then we have

\displaystyle[(sg)\alpha(s^{-1}),(sh)\alpha(s^{-1})]=\Big(\Big(\alpha(s)(g^{-1}s^{-1})\Big)\Big(\alpha(s)(h^{-1}s^{-1})\Big)\Big)\Big(\Big((sg)\alpha(s^{-1})\Big)\Big((sh)\alpha(s^{-1})\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\Big(\alpha^{2}(s)\Big((g^{-1}s^{-1})\Big(s\alpha^{-1}(h^{-1}s^{-1})\Big)\Big)\Big)\Big(\Big((sg)\alpha(s^{-1})\Big)\Big(\alpha(s)(hs^{-1})\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\Big(\alpha^{2}(s)\Big((g^{-1}s^{-1})\Big(\alpha^{-1}(sh^{-1})s^{-1}\Big)\Big)\Big(\alpha(sg)\Big(\alpha(s^{-1})(s\alpha^{-1}(hs^{-1}))\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\Big(\alpha^{2}(s)\Big(\alpha^{-1}((g^{-1}s^{-1})(sh^{-1}))\alpha(s^{-1})\Big)\Big)\Big(\alpha(sg)\Big((s^{-1}s)(hs^{-1})\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\Big(\alpha^{2}(s)\Big((g^{-1}h^{-1})\alpha(s^{-1})\Big)\Big)\Big(\alpha(sh)\alpha(hs^{-1})\Big)=\alpha^{3}(s)\Big(\Big((g^{-1}h^{-1})\alpha(s^{-1})\Big)\Big((sg)(hs^{-1})\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\alpha^{3}(s)\Big(\alpha(g^{-1}h^{-1})\Big(\alpha(s^{-1})\alpha^{-1}\Big((sg)(hs^{-1})\Big)\Big)\Big)=\alpha^{3}(s)\Big(\alpha(g^{-1}h^{-1})\Big(\Big(s^{-1}\alpha^{-1}(sg)\Big)(hs^{-1})\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\alpha^{3}(s)\Big(\alpha(g^{-1}h^{-1})\Big(\Big(\alpha^{-1}(s^{-1}s)g\Big)(hs^{-1})\Big)\Big)=\alpha^{3}(s)\Big(\alpha(g^{-1}h^{-1})\Big(\alpha(g)(hs^{-1})\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\alpha^{3}(s)\Big(\alpha(g^{-1}h^{-1})\Big((gh)\alpha(s^{-1})\Big)\Big)=\alpha^{3}(s)\Big(\Big((g^{-1}h^{-1})(gh)\Big)\alpha^{2}(s^{-1})\Big)=\alpha^{3}(s)\Big([g,h]\alpha^{2}(s^{-1})\Big)

\displaystyle=\Big(\alpha^{2}(s)[g,h]\Big)\alpha^{3}(s^{-1}).

So, we can notice that
(s[g,h])\alpha(s^{-1})=[(\alpha^{-2}(s)g)\alpha^{-1}(s^{-1}),(\alpha^{-2}(s)h)\alpha^{-1}(s^{-1})]
. This completes the proof.

Definition 3.16
.

Let
(G,\mu,e,\alpha)
be a regular Hom-group and
N=[G,G]
be the Commutator Hom-subgroup of
G
. We define the quotient Hom-group
(G^{ab}=G/N,\mu^{\prime},\widetilde{\alpha})
by

1.

\mu^{\prime}(gN,hN)=\mu(g,h)N,

2.

\widetilde{\alpha}(gN)=\alpha(g)N.

The Hom-group
G^{ab}
is called the Abelianization of the Hom-group
G.

Lemma 3.17
.

Let
(G,\mu,e,\alpha)
be a regular Hom-group and
N=[G,G]
be the Commutator subgroup of
G
. Then the Hom-group
(G^{ab},\mu^{\prime},\widetilde{\alpha})
is abelian. Moreover, if
H
is another normal Hom-subgroup and the quotient
G/H
is abelian, then
N\subset H.

Proof
.

From Lemma
1.11
, one can show that
(G^{ab},\mu^{\prime},\widetilde{\alpha})
is abelian. The proof of the second part is straightforward.

Lemma 3.18
.

Let
(G,\mu,e,\alpha)
be a regular Hom-group and
(G^{ab},\mu^{\prime},\widetilde{\alpha})
its Abelianization. Then the map
\pi:G\rightarrow G^{ab}
is an homomorphism of Hom-groups. It is called the canonical Hom-projection of
G
onto
G^{ab}.

Proof
.

The proof is straightforward.

Proposition 3.19
.

Let
(G,\mu_{G},e_{G},\alpha_{G})
be a regular Hom-group,
(G^{ab},\mu^{\prime},\widetilde{\alpha_{G}})
its Abelization and
(H,\mu_{H},e_{H},\alpha_{H})
be a regular abelain Hom-group. If
f:G\rightarrow H
is a Hom-group Homomorphism, then there exists a unique Hom-group homomorphism
\widetilde{f}:G^{ab}\rightarrow H
such that
\widetilde{f}\circ\pi=f.

Proof
.

We can deduce that
Ker(f)
is a normal Hom-subgroup of
G
. Moreover, one can check that
N\subset Ker(f).
This allows us to define a map
\widetilde{f}:G^{ab}\rightarrow H
by setting
\widetilde{f}(gN)=f(g).
To see that this is well-defined, suppose that
gN=g^{\prime}N
, so that
gg^{\prime-1}\in N\subset Ker(f)
. Then we have
f(g)f(g^{\prime})^{-1}=f(gg^{\prime-1})=e_{H}
, which implies that
f(g)=f(g^{\prime}).
Also note that
\widetilde{f}:G^{ab}\rightarrow H
is a homomorphism because

•

\widetilde{f}((gg^{\prime})N)=f(gg^{\prime})=f(g)f(g^{\prime})=\widetilde{f}(gN)\widetilde{f}(g^{\prime}N),

•

\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{\alpha_{G}}(gN))=\widetilde{f}(\alpha_{G}(g)N)=f(\alpha_{G}(g))=\alpha_{H}(f(g))=\alpha_{H}(\widetilde{f}(gN)),

and note that
\widetilde{f}\circ\pi=f
, since for all
g\in G
we have

\widetilde{f}\circ\pi(g)=\widetilde{f}(gN)=f(g).

Finally, suppose that
F:G^{ab}\rightarrow H
is another morphism satisfying
F\circ\pi=f.
Then for all
g\in G
we have

\widetilde{f}(gN)=f(g)=F(\pi(g))=F(gN).

So that
F=\widetilde{f}
as desired.

4
Simple Hom-groups

Definition 4.1
.

A Hom-group is simple if it is nontrivial and has no proper nontrivial
normal Hom-subgroups.

The following theorem gives a characterization of simple Hom-groups.

Theorem 4.2
.

Nontrivial simple Hom-groups are regular.

Proof
.

Let
(G,\mu,e,\alpha)
be a simple Hom-group. According to Corollary
3.9
, we have
ker(\alpha)\unrhd G
. Since
(G,\mu,e,\alpha)
is a simple Hom-group, then
Ker(\alpha)=\{e\}
or
Ker(\alpha)=G
. The Hom-group
G
is nontrivial, so
Ker(\alpha)\neq G
. Thus
\alpha
is bijective.

Definition 4.3
.

Given a Hom-group
(G,\mu,e,\alpha)
, a Hom-subgroup
H
is a maximal subgroup if:

1.

H\unrhd G,

2.

If
K\leq G
is a normal Hom-subgroup and
H\leq K
, then
K=H
or
K=G
, i.e., the only normal Hom-subgroup of
G
which contains
H
as a proper Hom-subgroup is
G.

Proposition 4.4
.

A normal Hom-subgroup
H
is a maximal Hom-subgroup if and only if
G/H
is a simple Hom-group.

Proof
.

We will start proving that if
H
is not maximal normal, then
G/H
is not simple. So, assume
H
is a normal Hom-subgroup which is not maximal, that is, there is
K\unrhd G
such that
H\lneq K\lneq G.
Let
\pi:G\rightarrow G/H
be the quotient map. Since
\pi
is a Hom-group homomorphism, then
\pi(K)
is normal. Since
H\lneq K\lneq G
, this is a nontrivial subgroup of
G/H
, showing that
G/H
is not simple.

Now, we prove the converse. Assume that
G/H
is not simple and let
\widetilde{K}
be a nontrivial normal Hom-subgroup of
G/H
. Now consider the set
K=\pi^{-1}(\widetilde{K})
. According to Proposition
3.10
, we have
K\unrhd G.
So it remains to claim that
K
contains
H
. Let
h\in H
, then
\alpha(h)\in H
. So
hH=eH
and therefore,
\pi(h)=eH
. Consequently, we have
h\in\pi^{-1}(eH)
and
H\subset\pi^{-1}(eH)\subset K.

5
Tensor product of regular Hom-groups

Definition 5.1
.

Let
(A,\alpha_{A})
and
(B,\alpha_{B})
be two hom-groups and
(C,\alpha_{C})
is another Hom-group (say in additive notation even if it is not assumed to be commutative). A map
f:A\times B\rightarrow C
from the cartesian product of any two Hom-groups
A
and
B
into a third
C
is called a Hom-bilinear map if;

1.

f(a_{1}a_{2},\alpha_{B}(b))=f(a_{1},b)+f(a_{2},b)
,

2.

f(\alpha_{A}(a),b_{1}b_{2})=f(a,b_{1})+f(a,b_{2})
,

3.

f(\alpha_{A}(a),\alpha_{B}(b))=\alpha_{C}\circ f(a,b)
,

for all
a,~a_{1},~a_{2}\in A
and
b,~b_{1},~b_{2}\in B
. The set of all such Hom-bilinear map from
A\times B
to
C
is denoted by
HL(A,B;C).

Remark 5.2
.

If
\alpha_{A}=\alpha_{B}=\alpha_{C}=Id,
we have the definition of a bilinear map.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
f
be a Hom-bilinear map from the Cartesian product of two regular Hom-groups
(A,\alpha_{A})
and
(B,\alpha_{B})
into a third regular Hom-group
(C,\alpha_{C})
(say in additive notation). Then

f(e_{A},b)=f(a,e_{b})=0,\;\forall a\in A\;\mbox{and}\;b\in B,

where
e_{A}
is the neutral element of
A
and
e_{B}
is the neutral element of
B.

Proof
.

For all
b\in B
, we have

\alpha_{C}(f(e_{A},b))=f(\alpha_{A}(e_{A}),\alpha_{B}(b))=f(e_{A}e_{A},\alpha_{B}(b))=f(e_{A},b)+f(e_{A},b).

Let
c
be the additive inverse of
f(e_{A},b)
in
C
.Then

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\alpha_{C}(c+f(e_{A},b))=\alpha_{C}(c)+\alpha_{C}(f(e_{A},b))=\alpha_{C}(c)+(f(e_{A},b)+f(e_{A},b))

\displaystyle=(c+f(e_{A},b))+\alpha_{C}(f(e_{A},b))=0+\alpha_{C}(f(e_{A},b))=\alpha^{2}_{C}(f(e_{A},b)).

Since
\alpha_{C}
is injective, we have
f(e_{A},b)=0.
The same holds for
f(a,e_{b})=0.

Corollary 5.4
.

Let
f:A\times B\rightarrow C
be a Hom-bilinear map from the Cartesian product of two regular Hom-groups
(A,\alpha_{A})
and
(B,\alpha_{B})
into a third regular Hom-group
(C,\alpha_{C})
(say in additive notation). Then for all
a\in A
and
b\in B
, we have
f(a^{-1},b)=-f(a,b)
and
f(a,b^{-1})=-f(a,b).

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
f
be a Hom-bilinear map from the Cartesian product of two regular Hom-groups
(A,\alpha_{A})
and
(B,\alpha_{B})
into a third regular Hom-group
(C,\alpha_{C})
(the product of
C
is denoted additively even if it is not commutative). Then, for all
a_{1},~a_{2}\in A
and
b_{1},~b_{2}\in B
, we have

f(a_{1},b_{2})+f(a_{2},b_{1})=f(a_{2},b_{1})+f(a_{1},b_{2}),

(5.1)

i.e., any two elements of the image of
f
commute.

Proof
.

Let
a_{1},~a_{2}\in A
and
b_{1},~b_{2}\in B
. Then we have

\displaystyle f(\alpha_{A}^{2}(a_{1}+a_{2}),\alpha_{B}^{2}(b_{1}+b_{2}))

\displaystyle=f(\alpha_{A}^{2}(a_{1}+a_{2}),\alpha_{B}^{2}(b_{1})+\alpha_{B}^{2}(b_{2}))

\displaystyle=f(\alpha_{A}(a_{1}+a_{2}),\alpha_{B}^{2}(b_{1}))+f(\alpha_{A}(a_{1}+a_{2}),\alpha_{B}^{2}(b_{2}))

\displaystyle=\Big(f(\alpha_{A}(a_{1}),\alpha_{B}(b_{1}))+f(\alpha_{A}(a_{2}),\alpha_{B}(b_{1}))\Big)

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\Big(f(\alpha_{A}(a_{1}),\alpha_{B}(b_{2}))+f(\alpha_{A}(a_{2}),\alpha_{B}(b_{2}))\Big)

\displaystyle=\alpha_{C}\Big(\Big(f(a_{1},b_{1})+f(a_{2},b_{1})\Big)+\Big(f(a_{1},b_{2})+f(a_{2},b_{2})\Big)\Big).

On other hand we have

\displaystyle f(\alpha_{A}^{2}(a_{1}+a_{2}),\alpha_{B}^{2}(b_{1}+b_{2}))

\displaystyle=f(\alpha_{A}^{2}(a_{1})+\alpha_{A}^{2}(a_{2})),\alpha_{B}^{2}(b_{1}+b_{2}))

\displaystyle=f(\alpha_{A}^{2}(a_{1}),\alpha_{B}(b_{1}+b_{2}))+f(\alpha_{A}^{2}(a_{2}),\alpha_{B}(b_{1}+b_{2}))

\displaystyle=\Big(f(\alpha_{A}(a_{1}),\alpha_{B}(b_{1}))+f(\alpha_{A}(a_{1}),\alpha_{B}(b_{2}))\Big)

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\Big(f(\alpha_{A}(a_{2}),\alpha_{B}(b_{1}))+f(\alpha_{A}(a_{2}),\alpha_{B}(b_{2}))\Big)

\displaystyle=\alpha_{C}\Big(\Big(f(a_{1},b_{1})+f(a_{1},b_{2})\Big)+\Big(f(a_{2},b_{1})+f(a_{2},b_{2})\Big)\Big).

So, it follows from Lemma
1.9
that
f(a_{1},b_{2})+f(a_{2},b_{1})=f(a_{2},b_{1})+f(a_{1},b_{2}).

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
f
be a Hom-bilinear map from the Cartesian product of two regular Hom-groups
(A,\alpha_{A})
and
(B,\alpha_{B})
into a third regular Hom-group
(C,\alpha_{C})
(the product of
C
is denoted additively even if it is not commutative). Then,

1.

for all
b\in B
, the kernel of the map
f(\cdot,b):a\in A\rightarrow f(a,b)\in C
contains
[A,A]
,

2.

for all
a\in A
, the kernel of the map
f(a,\cdot):b\in B\rightarrow f(a,b)\in C
contains
[B,B]
.

Proof
.

By straightforward calculation, we have

f([a_{1},a_{2}],b)=-(f(a_{1},\alpha_{B}^{-2}(b))+f(a_{2},\alpha_{B}^{-2}(b)))+(f(a_{1},\alpha_{B}^{-2}(b))+f(a_{2},\alpha_{B}^{-2}(b))),\;\forall[a_{1},a_{2}]\in[A,A],\ \ b\in B,

and

f(a,[b_{1},b_{2}])=-(f(\alpha_{A}^{-2}(a),b_{1})+f(\alpha_{A}^{-2}(a),b_{2}))+(f(\alpha_{A}^{-2}(a),b_{1})+f(\alpha_{A}^{-2}(a),b_{2})),\;\forall[b_{1},b_{2}]\in[B,B],\ \ a\in A.

Definition 5.7
.

Let
(A,\alpha_{A})
and
(B,\alpha_{B})
be two regular Hom-groups.
A tensor product of
A
and
B
is a regular Hom-group
(A\otimes B,\alpha_{A\otimes B})
which is equipped with a Hom-bilinear map

\theta:A\times B\rightarrow A\otimes B,

such that, for every regular Hom-group
(C,\alpha_{C})
and every Hom-bilinear map
f:A\times B\rightarrow C
, there exists a unique Hom-group homomorphism
\widetilde{f}:A\otimes B\rightarrow C
such that the diagram:

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 16.90448pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-16.90448pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\times B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 78.76697pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 22.0711pt\raise-9.52779pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 40.90448pt\raise-26.74945pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 69.7562pt\raise-22.25pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.61111pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\widetilde{f}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 54.76697pt\raise-26.74269pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise
