0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 40.90448pt\raise-31.27777pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

commutes, that is
\widetilde{f}\circ\theta=f.

Proposition 5.8
.

Let
(A,\alpha_{A})
and
(B,\alpha_{B})
be two regular Hom-groups. Tensor products of
A
and
B
are unique up to unique isomorphism.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
(A,\alpha_{A})
and
(B,\alpha_{B})
be two Hom-groups. We have
(A\otimes B,\theta,\alpha_{A\otimes B})\cong(B\otimes A,\theta^{\prime},\alpha_{B\otimes A}).

Proof
.

Defining the maps

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}f&:&A\times B&\to&B\times A\\
&&(a,b)&\mapsto&f(a,b)=(b,a)\\
\end{array},\ \ \ \begin{array}[]{ccccc}g&:&B\times A&\to&A\times B\\
&&(b,a)&\mapsto&f(b,a)=(a,b)\end{array},

we have
f\circ(\alpha_{A}\times\alpha_{B})=(\alpha_{B}\times\alpha_{A})\circ f
and
g\circ(\alpha_{B}\times\alpha_{A})=(\alpha_{A}\times\alpha_{B})\circ g.
From the universel property of
\theta
and
\theta^{\prime}
, we have the following diagram:

\textstyle{A\times B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{{B\times A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{\theta^{\prime}}
\textstyle{{A\times B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\textstyle{{A\otimes B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{{B\otimes A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{\prime}}
\textstyle{{A\otimes B}}

The Hom-bilinearity of
\theta^{\prime}\circ f
and
\theta\circ g
ensure the existence of homomorphisms
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
. Now
g\circ f
is the identity map of
A\times B
, hence
g^{\prime}\circ f^{\prime}
is the identity map of
A\otimes B
. Similarly
f^{\prime}\circ g^{\prime}
is the identity of
B\otimes A
, thus
(A\otimes B,\theta,\alpha_{A\otimes B})\cong(B\otimes A,\theta^{\prime},\alpha_{B\otimes A}).

Proposition 5.10
.

Let
(A,\alpha_{A})
and
(B,\alpha_{B})
be two regular Hom-groups. Then
A\otimes B\cong A^{ab}\times B^{ab}
. In particular,
A\otimes B
is a regular abelian Hom-group.

Proof
.

According to Lemma
5.6
, one can show that any Hom-bilinear map
f:A\times B\rightarrow C
from the Cartesian product of two regular Hom-groups
(A,\alpha_{A})
and
(B,\alpha_{B})
into a third regular Hom-group
(C,\alpha_{C})
is determined by a unique Hom-bilinear
f^{\prime}:A^{ab}\times B^{ab}\rightarrow C
such that
f=f^{\prime}\circ\gamma
, where
\gamma:A\times B\rightarrow A^{ab}\times B^{ab}
is the canonical projection map.

6
Hom-type rings

In this section, we introduce Hom-rings and their properties.

6.1
Definitions and properties

The word Hom-ring was mentioned for the first time by A. Gohr and Y. Fregier in
[
5
]
.
They defined Hom-rings as follows.
Let
A
be a set together with two binary operations
+:A\times A\rightarrow A
and
\cdot:A\times A\rightarrow A
, one self-map
\alpha:A\rightarrow A
and a special element
0\in A
. Then
(A,+,\cdot,0,\alpha)
is called a Hom-ring if:

•

(A,+,0)
is an abelian group.

•

The multiplication is distributive on both sides.

•

\alpha
is an abelian group homomorphism.

•

\alpha
and
\cdot
satisfy the Hom-associativity condition;
\alpha(x)\cdot(y\cdot z)=(x\cdot y)\cdot\alpha(z)
.

In this section, we define different types of Hom-rings based on Hom-groups and provide various ways of constructing them.

Definition 6.1
.

A Hom-ring of type
(1)
is a tuple
(A,+,\cdot,0,\alpha,\beta)
consisting of a set
A
together with two binary operations
+:A\times A\rightarrow A
(the addition) and
\cdot:A\times A\rightarrow A
(the multiplication) and two set maps
\alpha,\beta:A\rightarrow A
, such that:

1.

(A,+,0,\alpha)
is an abelian Hom-group.

2.

\beta
is an endomorphism of the abelian Hom-group
(A,+,0,\alpha)
, i.e,
\beta(x+y)=\beta(x)+\beta(y),\;\forall x,~y\in A,
and
\alpha\circ\beta=\beta\circ\alpha.

3.

\alpha
and
\beta
are multiplicative maps , i.e,
\alpha(xy)=\alpha(x)\alpha(y)
and
\beta(xy)=\beta(x)\beta(y)
, for all
x,~y\in A.

4.

The map
\beta
and the product
\cdot
satisfy the Hom-associativity condition;

\beta(x)\cdot(y\cdot z)=(x\cdot y)\cdot\beta(z).

(6.1)

5.

The multiplication is Hom-distributive over the addition on both sides;

(a)

\alpha(x)\cdot(y+z)=x\cdot y+x\cdot z,

(6.2)

(b)

(y+z)\cdot\alpha(x)=y\cdot x+z\cdot x,

(6.3)

for all
x,~y,~z\in A.

If
(A,+,\cdot,0,\alpha,\beta)
admits a unit element
1\in A
satisfying the following properties

1.

x\cdot 1=1\cdot x=\beta(x),~\forall x\in A,

(6.4)

2.

\alpha(1)=\beta(1)=1,

(6.5)

the Hom-ring
A
is said to be unitary.

Remark 6.2
.

Note that if
\alpha=id
, we automatically have the definition of Hom-ring in
[
5
]
.

When
\alpha=\beta
, a Hom-ring of type
(1)
should be reffered to as ”
\alpha
-Hom-ring of type
(1)
”.

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
(A,+,\cdot,0,\alpha,\beta)
be a tuple, where
(A,+,\cdot,0)
is an abelian Hom-group and
\alpha
is a multiplicative map. If
(A,+,\cdot,0,\alpha,\beta)
satisfies (
6.1
), (
6.2
) or (
6.3
), (
6.4
) and (
6.5
) , then
\beta
is an endomorphism of the abelian hom-group
(A,+,0,\alpha)
and a multiplicative map.

Proof
.

Suppose that
(A,+,\cdot,0,\alpha,\beta)
satisfies (
6.1
), (
6.2
), (
6.4
) and (
6.5
), so for any
x,~y\in A
, we have

\displaystyle\beta(x+y)=1\cdot(x+y)=\alpha(1)\cdot(x+y)=1\cdot x+1\cdot y=\beta(x)+\beta(y),

and

\displaystyle\alpha\circ\beta(x)=\alpha(\beta(x))=\alpha(1\cdot x)=\alpha(1)\cdot\alpha(x)=1\cdot\alpha(x)=\beta(\alpha(x)).

The multiplicativity of
\beta
is obtained from (
6.1
)

\displaystyle\beta(x\cdot y)=(x\cdot y)\cdot 1=(x\cdot y)\cdot\beta(1)=\beta(x)\cdot(y\cdot 1)=\beta(x)\beta(y).

Remark 6.4
.

As a consequence of the previous proposition, a unitary Hom-ring of type
(1)
can be defined as a tuple
(A,+,\cdot,0,\alpha,\beta)
such that
(A,+,0,\alpha)
is an abelian Hom-group,
\alpha
is a multiplicative map and
(A,+,\cdot,0,\alpha,\beta)
satisfies the identities (
6.1
), (
6.2
), (
6.3
), (
6.4
) and (
6.5
).

Now, we are going to introduce another type of Hom-ring as follows

Definition 6.5
.

A Hom-ring of type
(2)
is a tuple
(A,+,\cdot,0,\alpha,\beta)
consisting of a set
A
together with two binary operations
+:A\times A\rightarrow A
(the addition) and
\cdot:A\times A\rightarrow A
(the multiplication) and two set maps
\alpha,\beta:A\rightarrow A
, such that:

1.

(A,+,0,\alpha)
is an abelian Hom-group.

2.

\beta
is an endomorphism of the abelian Hom-group
(A,+,0,\alpha)
, i.e.,
\beta(x+y)=\beta(x)+\beta(y),\;\forall x,~y\in A,
and
\alpha\circ\beta=\beta\circ\alpha.

3.

\alpha
and
\beta
are multiplicative maps, i.e.,
\alpha(xy)=\alpha(x)\alpha(y)
and
\beta(xy)=\beta(x)\beta(y)
, for all
x,~y\in A.

4.

The maps
\alpha,~\beta
and the product
\cdot
satisfy
\beta(\alpha^{2}(x))\cdot(\beta(\alpha(y))\cdot\beta^{2}(z))=(\alpha^{2}(x)\cdot\beta(\alpha(y)))\cdot\beta^{2}(\alpha(z)).

5.

The multiplication is Hom-distributive over the addition on both sides;

(a)

\alpha^{2}(x)\cdot\beta(y+z)=\alpha(x)\cdot\beta(y)+\alpha(x)\cdot\beta(z)
,

(b)

\alpha(y+z)\cdot\alpha(\beta(x))=\alpha(y)\cdot\beta(x)+\alpha(z)\cdot\beta(x),

for all
x,~y,~z\in A.

If
(A,+,\cdot,0,\alpha,\beta)
admits a unit element
1\in A
satisfying the following identities

1.

x\cdot 1=\beta(x)
and
1\cdot x=\alpha(x),~\forall x\in A,

2.

\alpha(1)=\beta(1)=1,

the Hom-ring
A
is said to be unitary.

Definition 6.6
.

Let
(A,+,\cdot,0,\alpha,\beta)
be a Hom-ring of type
(2)
. It is said to be an ”
\alpha
-Hom-ring of type
(2)
” if
\alpha=\beta.

Definition 6.7
.

A Hom-ring
(A,+,\cdot,0,\alpha,\beta)
is called a commutative Hom-ring if for all
x,~y\in A
we have
x\cdot y=y\cdot x
. Also, a Hom-ring is said to be regular if the twist maps
\alpha
and
\beta
are bijective. The inverse of an element
x
in a Hom-ring
(A,+,\cdot,0,\alpha,\beta)
with respect to the addition is named the additive inverse and it is denoted by
(-x)
.

Now we have introduced these two types of Hom-ring, it’s natural to seek for relations among them and provide a way for constructing them.

Lemma 6.8
.

Let
(A,+,\cdot,0,\alpha,\beta)
be a Hom-ring of type
(1)
. One has for all
x,~y,~z\in A
:

1.

\beta(\alpha^{2}(x))\cdot(\beta(\alpha(y))\cdot\beta^{2}(z))=\alpha(\alpha(x)\cdot\beta(y))\cdot\beta^{3}(z).

2.

(\alpha^{2}(x)\cdot\beta(\alpha(y)))\cdot\beta^{2}(\alpha(z))=\beta\circ\alpha(\alpha(x)\cdot(y\cdot z)).

3.

\alpha^{2}(x)\cdot\beta(y+z)=\alpha(x)\cdot\beta(y)+\alpha(x)\cdot\beta(z).

4.

\alpha(y+z)\cdot\alpha(\beta(x))=\alpha(y)\cdot\beta(x)+\alpha(z)\cdot\beta(x).

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
(A,+,\cdot,0,\alpha,\beta)
be a regular Hom-ring of type
(2)
. Then, for all
x,~y,~z\in A
:

1.

\beta(x)\cdot(y\cdot z)=(x\cdot y)\cdot\alpha(z).

2.

(x\cdot y)\cdot\beta(z)=\beta(x)\cdot(y\beta(\alpha^{-1}(z)))
.

3.

\alpha(x)\cdot(y+z)=x\cdot y+x\cdot z
.

4.

(y+z)\cdot\alpha(x)=y\cdot x+z\cdot x.

The desired equivalence is then a simple corollary of this two lemmas:

Proposition 6.10
.

Regular
\alpha
-Hom-rings of type
(1)
and
(2)
are equivalent.

The following provides a source of examples for Hom-ring;

Proposition 6.11
.

Let
(A,+,\cdot,0)
be a ring and
\alpha,\beta:A\rightarrow A
two commuting homomorphism of of rings. Defining a new addition
\widetilde{+}:A\times A\rightarrow A
and a new multiplication
\widetilde{\cdot}:A\times A\rightarrow A
given by

•

x\widetilde{+}y=\alpha(x+y)=\alpha(x)+\alpha(y),

•

x\widetilde{\cdot}y=\beta(x\cdot y)=\beta(x)\cdot\beta(y),
for all
x,~y\in A
,

then
(A,\widetilde{+},\widetilde{\cdot},0,\alpha,\beta)
is a Hom-ring of type
(1).
We denote this by
A_{\alpha,\beta}
and it is called the twist ring of
A.

Proof
.

The proof is straightforward and left to the reader.

Proposition 6.12
.

Let
(A,+,\cdot,0,\alpha,\beta)
be a regular Hom-ring of type
(1)
. Defining a new addition
+^{\prime}:A\times A\rightarrow A
and a new multiplication
\cdot^{\prime}:A\times A\rightarrow A
given by

•

x+^{\prime}y=\alpha^{-1}(x+y)=\alpha^{-1}(x)+\alpha^{-1}(y),

•

x\cdot^{\prime}y=\beta^{-1}(x\cdot y)=\beta^{-1}(x)\cdot\beta^{-1}(y),
for all
x,~y\in A
,

then
(A,+^{\prime},\cdot^{\prime},0)
is a ring. It is called the compatible ring of the Hom-ring
(A,+,\cdot,0,\alpha,\beta)
.

Proposition 6.13
.

Let
(A,+,\cdot,0)
be a ring and
\alpha,\beta:A\rightarrow A
two commuting homomorphism of rings. Defining a new addition
\widetilde{+}:A\times A\rightarrow A
and a new multiplication
\widetilde{\cdot}:A\times A\rightarrow A
given by

•

x\widetilde{+}y=\alpha(x+y)=\alpha(x)+\alpha(y),

•

x\widetilde{\cdot}y=\beta(x)\cdot\alpha(y)
, for all
x,~y\in A
,

then
(A,\widetilde{+},\widetilde{\cdot},0,\alpha,\beta)
is a Hom-ring of type
(2).
We denote this by
A_{\alpha,\beta}
and it is called the twist ring of
A.

Proposition 6.14
.

Let
(A,+,\cdot,0,\alpha,\beta)
be a regular Hom-ring of type
(2)
. Defining a new addition
+^{\prime}:A\times A\rightarrow A
and a new multiplication
\cdot^{\prime}:A\times A\rightarrow A
given by

•

x+^{\prime}y=\alpha^{-1}(x+y)=\alpha^{-1}(x)+\alpha^{-1}(y),

•

x\cdot^{\prime}y=\beta^{-1}(x)\cdot\alpha^{-1}(y),
for all
x,~y\in A
,

then
(A,+^{\prime},\cdot^{\prime},0)
is a ring, which is called the compatible ring of the Hom-ring
(A,+,\cdot,0,\alpha,\beta)
.

In what follows we provide some properties and define Hom-ring homomorphisms and Hom-subrings.

Proposition 6.15
.

Let
(A,+,\cdot,0,\alpha,\beta)
be a unitary regular hom-ring of type
(1)
or of type
(2).

1.

For all
a\in A
, we have
a\cdot 0=0\cdot a=0.

2.

Let
a,~b\in A
. Then , the additive inverse of
(a\cdot b)
is
(-a)\cdot b=a\cdot(-b)=-(a\cdot b)
. One can notes that
\alpha(-a)=-\alpha(a).

Proof
.

We have

0\cdot\alpha(a)=(0+0)\cdot\alpha(a)=0\cdot a+0\cdot a.

Let
c
be the additive inverse of
0\cdot a
, i.e.,
c+0\cdot a=0
. Then we have

\displaystyle 0=\alpha(c+0\cdot a)=\alpha(c)+0\cdot\alpha(a)=\alpha(c)+(0\cdot a+0\cdot a))=(c+0\cdot a)+\alpha(0\cdot a)=\alpha^{2}(0\cdot a).

Since
\alpha
is injective, then
0\cdot a=0
. The second property is a consequence of the first property.

Definition 6.16
.

Let
(A,+_{A},\cdot_{A},0_{A},\alpha_{A},\beta_{A})
and
(B,+_{B},\cdot_{B},0_{B},\alpha_{B},\beta_{B})
be two unitary hom-rings.

•

A homomorphism of Hom-rings is a map
f:A\rightarrow B
such that

1.

f(0_{A})=0_{B}
and
f(1_{A})=1_{B}

2.

f(x+_{A}y)=f(x)+_{B}f(y)

3.

f(x\cdot_{A}y)=f(x)\cdot_{B}f(y).

4.

f\circ\alpha_{A}=\alpha_{B}\circ f
, and
f\circ\beta_{A}=\beta_{B}\circ f.

•

The map
f
is called a weak homomorphism if it satisfies the second and the third condition.

•

The Hom-rings
(A,+_{A},\cdot_{A},0_{A},\alpha_{A},\beta_{A})
and
(B,+_{B},\cdot_{B},0_{B},\alpha_{B},\beta_{B})
are called isomorphic if
f
is bijective.

•

If
f
is the inclusion map, we say that
(A,+,\cdot,0_{A},\alpha,\beta)
is a Hom-subring of
(B,+,\cdot,0_{B},\alpha,\beta).

Remark 6.17
.

Let
(A,+,\cdot,0_{A},\alpha,\beta)
be a Hom-subring of
(B,+,\cdot,0_{B},\alpha,\beta)
. Then for all
x\in A
, we have (obviously)
\alpha(x)\in A
and
\beta(x)\in A
. Also, a Hom-subring of a Hom-ring
(A,+,\cdot,0_{A},\alpha,\beta)
is a Hom-subgroup of
(A,+,0_{A},\alpha)
stable by the multiplication and the map
\beta
.

Definition 6.18
.

Let
(A,+,\cdot,0,\alpha,\beta)
be a unitary regular Hom-ring of type
(1)
(resp. regular Hom-ring of type
(2)
). The center
Z(A)
(resp. center
Z^{\prime}(A)
) of
A
is the set of all
x\in A
where
x\cdot a=a\cdot x
(resp.
x\cdot\beta(a)=\alpha(a)\cdot x
), for all
a\in A
.

Proposition 6.19
.

Let
(A,+,\cdot,0,\alpha)
be a unitary regular Hom-ring.

1.

If
(A,+,\cdot,0,\alpha)
is of type
(1)
, then
Z(A)
is a Hom-subring of
A
.

2.

If
(A,+,\cdot,0,\alpha)
is of type
(2)
, then
Z^{\prime}(A)
is a Hom-subring of
A
.

Proof
.

1.

Let
(A,+,\cdot,0,\alpha)
be a Hom-ring of type
(1)

We have, for all
a\in A
,
0\cdot a=a\cdot 0=0
and
1\cdot a=a\cdot 1=\beta(a)
. Then
0
and
1
are in
Z(A).

Let
x,~y\in Z(A)
. Then for all
a\in A
, we have

(a)

(x+y)\cdot a=(x+y)\cdot\alpha(\alpha^{-1}(a))=x\cdot\alpha^{-1}(a)+y\cdot\alpha^{-1}(a)=\alpha^{-1}(a)\cdot x+\alpha^{-1}(a)\cdot y=a\cdot(x+y).

Thus
Z(A)
is closed under addition of
A
.

(b)

If
x\in Z(A)
and
a\in A
then
-(x\cdot a)=-(a\cdot x)
. Furthermore, we have
-(x\cdot a)=(-x)\cdot a
and

-(a\cdot x)=a\cdot(-x).
So
-x\in Z(A)
.

(c)

(x\cdot y)\cdot a=(x\cdot y)\cdot\alpha(\alpha^{-1}(a))=\alpha(x)\cdot(y\cdot\alpha^{-1}(a))=\alpha(x)\cdot(\alpha^{-1}(a)\cdot y)=(x\cdot\alpha^{-1}(a))\cdot\alpha(y)=(\alpha^{-1}(a)\cdot x)\cdot\alpha(y)=a\cdot(x\cdot y).

Thus
Z(A)
is closed under multiplication of
A
.

2.

Let
(A,+,\cdot,0,\alpha)
be a Hom-ring of type
(2)
.
We have, for all
a\in A
,
0\cdot\beta(a)=\alpha(a)\cdot 0=0
and
1\cdot\beta(a)=\alpha(a)\cdot 1=\beta\circ\alpha(a)
. Then
0
and
1
are in
Z^{\prime}(A).

Let
x,~y\in Z^{\prime}(A)
. Then for all
a\in A
, we have

(a)

(x+y)\cdot\beta(a)=\alpha^{-1}(\alpha^{-1}(x)+\alpha^{-1}(y))\cdot\beta(\alpha(\alpha^{-1}(a)))=x\cdot\beta(\alpha^{-1}(a))+y\cdot\beta(\alpha^{-1}(a))=a\cdot x+a\cdot y=\alpha(a)\cdot(x+y).

Thus
Z^{\prime}(A)
is closed under addition of
A
.

(b)

If
x\in Z^{\prime}(A)
and
a\in A
then
-(x\cdot a)=-(a\cdot x)
. Furthermore, we have
-(x\cdot a)=(-x)\cdot a
and

-(a\cdot x)=a\cdot(-x).
Then
-x\in Z^{\prime}(A).

(c)

(x\cdot y)\cdot\beta(a)=\beta(x)\cdot(y\cdot\beta(\alpha^{-1}(a)))=\beta(x)\cdot(a\cdot y)=(x\cdot a)\cdot\alpha(y)=\alpha(a)\cdot(x\cdot y).

Thus
Z^{\prime}(A)
is closed under multiplication of
A
.

6.2
Examples

Proposition 6.20
.

Let
(A,+,\alpha_{A})
be a regular abelian Hom-group and consider the group homomorphisms from
A
into
A
. Then
(End(A),+,\circ,0,\alpha_{End(A)},id)
is a Hom-ring of type
(1)
such that

1.

The addition of two such homomorphisms may be defined pointwise to produce another group homomorphism. Explicitly, given two such homomorphisms
f
and
g
, the sum of
f
and
g
is the homomorphism
(f+g)(x)=f(x)+g(x)
,

2.

The composition
\circ
of two such homomorphisms
f
and
g
is explicitly
(f\circ g)(x)=f(g(x))
,

3.

\alpha_{End(A)}(f)=\alpha_{A}\circ f
.

Proof
.

The proof is straightforward and left to the reader.

Polynomial Hom-Rings:
Let
A
be a ring and
R=A[X_{1},\cdot\cdot\cdot,X_{n}]
be the polynomial ring in
n
variables.

Let us recall that any element of
R
is uniquely written as a linear combination, with coefficients in
A
, of monomes
X^{n}=X_{1}^{m_{1}}...X_{n}^{m_{n}}
, where
m
runs over the set of sequences of
n
nonnegative integers.

Take a ring endomorphism of
A
,
\alpha:A\rightarrow A\subset R.
Then a ring endomorphism
\widetilde{\alpha}
of R is uniquely determined by the

n
polynomials
\widetilde{\alpha}(X_{i})=\sum_{r}a_{i,r}X^{r}=\sum_{r}a_{i,r}X_{1}^{r_{1}}...X_{n}^{r_{n}}
for
1\leq i\leq n.

Let

\widetilde{\alpha}(P)=\sum_{m}\alpha(a_{m})\widetilde{\alpha}(X_{1})^{m_{1}}...\widetilde{\alpha}(X_{n})^{m_{n}},
for
P=\sum_{m}a_{m}X_{1}^{m_{1}}...X_{n}^{m_{n}}\in R.

For
P=\sum_{m}a_{m}X_{1}^{m_{1}}...X_{n}^{m_{n}}
and
Q=\sum_{m}b_{m}X_{1}^{m_{1}}...X_{n}^{m_{n}}
, define
\widehat{+}
by

P\widehat{+}Q=\sum_{m}\alpha(a_{m}+b_{m})\widetilde{\alpha}(X_{1})^{m_{1}}...\widetilde{\alpha}(X_{n})^{m_{n}},

and for
P=\sum_{m}a_{m}X_{1}^{m_{1}}...X_{n}^{m_{n}}
and
Q=\sum_{l}b_{l}X_{1}^{l_{1}}...X_{n}^{l_{n}}
, define
\widehat{\cdot}
by

P\widehat{\cdot}Q=\sum_{d}\sum_{m+l=d}\alpha(a_{m})\alpha(b_{l})\widetilde{\alpha}(X_{1})^{d_{1}}...\widetilde{\alpha}(X_{n})^{d_{n}}.

Then
R_{\widetilde{\alpha}}=(R,\widehat{+},\widehat{\cdot},\widetilde{\alpha})
is a
\alpha-
Hom-ring of type
(1)
.

Twisted Group-Ring:
Let
G
be a group and
\mathbb{K}G
the corresponding group-ring over
\mathbb{K}
. the set
\mathbb{K}G
is generated by
\{e_{g}:g\in G\}
.If
\alpha:G\rightarrow G
is a group homomorphism, then it can be extended to a ring endomorphism of
\mathbb{K}G
by setting

\alpha\Big(\sum_{g\in G}a_{g}e_{g}\Big)=\sum_{g\in G}a_{g}\alpha(e_{g})=\sum_{g\in G}a_{g}e_{\alpha(g)}.

Consider the usual ring structure on
\mathbb{K}G
. Then, we define a
\alpha-
Hom-ring of type
(1),~(\mathbb{K}G,\widehat{+},\widehat{\cdot},\widetilde{\alpha})
over
\mathbb{K}G
by setting:

\displaystyle\sum_{g\in G}a_{g}e_{g}\widehat{+}\sum_{g\in G}b_{g}e_{g}=\sum_{g\in G}(a_{g}+b_{g})e_{\alpha(g)},\ \ \ (\sum_{g\in G}a_{g}e_{g})\widehat{\cdot}(\sum_{h\in G}b_{h}e_{h})=\sum_{gh\in G}a_{g}b_{g}e_{\alpha(gh)}.

7
Modules over
\alpha
-Hom-rings of type
(1)

We introduce in the following the structure of module over
\alpha
-Hom-ring of type
(1).

The properties of modules for other types Hom-ring will be discussed in a forthcoming paper.

In the rest of this article, the word Hom-ring means an
\alpha
-Hom-ring of type
(1).

7.1
Definitions and Properties

Definition 7.1
.

Let
(A,+_{A},\cdot,0,\alpha)
be a unitary Hom-ring with unit
1_{A}
. A left
A
-module
M
consists of an abelian Hom-group
(M,+_{M},\beta)
and an operation
\lambda_{l}:A\otimes M\rightarrow M
such that for all
a,~b
in
A
and
m_{1},~m_{2}
in
M
, we have:

\lambda_{l}(\alpha(a)\otimes(m_{1}+_{M}m_{2}))=\lambda_{l}(a\otimes m_{1})+_{M}\lambda_{l}(a\otimes m_{1}),

(7.1)

\lambda_{l}((a+_{A}b)\otimes\beta_{M}(m_{1}))=\lambda_{l}(a\otimes m_{1})+_{M}\lambda_{l}(b\otimes m_{1}),

(7.2)

\lambda_{l}((a\cdot b)\otimes\beta(m_{1}))=\lambda_{l}(\alpha(a)\otimes\lambda_{l}(b\otimes m),

(7.3)

\lambda_{l}(1_{A}\otimes m_{1})=\beta(m_{1}).

(7.4)

The operation of the Hom-ring on
M
is called scalar multiplication, and it is usually written as
am
for
a
in
A
and
m
in
M
. The notation
{}_{A}M
indicates a left
A
-module
M
. If
\beta
is invertible, such left module
{}_{A}M
is said regular. A right
A
-module
M
or
M_{A}
is defined similarly, except that the ring acts on the right; i.e., the operation takes the form
\lambda_{r}:M\otimes A\rightarrow M
, and the above axioms are written with a scalar multiplication on the right.

Example 7.2
.

If
A
is a unitary Hom-ring, then we may consider
A
as either a left or right A-module, where the action is given by the multiplication.

Proposition 7.3
.

Let
(A,+_{A},\cdot,0,\alpha)
be a unitary Hom-ring. If
A
is commutative, then these left modules are the same as right modules.

Proof
.

Let
M
be a right
A
-module, i.e., there exists
\lambda_{r}:M\otimes A\longrightarrow M
by
\lambda_{r}(m\otimes a)=ma
. Now, we define
\lambda_{l}:A\otimes M\longrightarrow M
by
\lambda_{l}(a\otimes m)=ma
. Using the commutativity of the Hom-ring
A
, we can verify that
\lambda_{l}
defines a left module structure for
M
over
A.

Definition 7.4
.

If
R
and
S
are two Hom-rings, then an
R-S
-bimodule is an abelian Hom- group
M
such that:

1.

M
is a left
R
-module and a right
S
-module,

2.

(rm)\alpha_{S}(s)=\alpha_{R}(r)(ms)
.

Definition 7.5
.

A bimodule is both a left
A
-module with action
\lambda_{l}
and
a right
A
-module with action
\lambda_{r}
satisfying the compatibility condition

\lambda_{r}\circ(\lambda_{l}\otimes\alpha)=\lambda_{l}\circ(\alpha\otimes\lambda_{r}).

(7.5)

It is simply called
A
-module.

Proposition 7.6
.

Let
(A,+_{A},\cdot,0_{A},\alpha)
be a unitary Hom-ring and
(M,+_{M},0_{M},\beta)
be a regular left
A
-module.

1.

For all
m
in
M
, we have
0_{A}m=0_{M}.

2.

If moreover
a\in A
admits an additive inverse of index
r\leq n
, then the additive inverse of
am
on
M
is
-(am)=(-a)m=a(-m)
. We can observe that
\beta(-m)=-\beta(m)
.

Proof
.

We can show that the first property in the same way as the first property of Proposition
Proof
. For the second property, we have

\alpha^{n-1}(a)\beta^{n}(m)+\alpha^{n-1}(-a)\beta^{n}(m)=\alpha^{n}(a+(-a))\beta^{n+1}(m)=0\beta^{n-1}(m)=0.

In the same way we check the other identity.

Remark 7.7
.

If
M
is a module over a Hom-ring
A
, then two equations (
7.3
) and (
7.4
) give that

\beta(am)=\alpha(a)\beta(m).

(7.6)

One can get, for all
m
element in right (regular)
A
-module
M
,
m0_{A}=0_{M}
.

7.2
Module Constructions

Proposition 7.8
.

Let
(A,+_{A},\cdot_{A},0,\alpha)
be a unitary regular Hom-ring,
(M,+_{M},\beta)
a regular
A
-module (left),
(A,+_{A}^{\prime},\cdot^{\prime})
the compatible ring. If
\begin{array}[]{l|rcl}\lambda_{l}:&A&\longrightarrow&End(M)\\
&a&\longmapsto&\lambda_{l}(a),\end{array}
is the action of the Hom-ring
A
on
M
, then
(M,+_{M}^{\prime}=\beta^{-1}\circ+_{M})
is a (left)
(A,+_{A}^{\prime},\cdot^{\prime})
-module with respect the action
\lambda_{l}^{\prime}=\beta^{-1}\lambda_{l}.

Proof
.

For the action
\lambda_{l}^{\prime}
, the conditions for
(M,+_{M}^{\prime})
to be an
(A,+_{A}^{\prime},\cdot^{\prime})
-module is written in the form

\beta^{-1}(a(m_{1}+_{M}m_{2}))=\beta^{-2}(am_{1}+_{M}am_{2}),

(7.7)

\beta^{-1}((a+_{A}b)m_{1}))=\beta^{-2}(am_{1}+_{M}bm_{1}),

(7.8)

\beta^{-1}(a\beta^{-1}(bm_{1})=\beta^{-1}(\alpha^{-1}(a\cdot_{A}b)m_{1}),

(7.9)

\beta^{-1}(1_{A}m_{1})=m_{1}.

(7.10)

Also, one can check that equation (
7.6
) is equivalent to

\lambda_{l}\alpha(a)=\beta\lambda_{l}(a)\beta^{-1}.

(7.11)

Then the result can easily follow.

From Proposition
7.8
, we can get a method of building (left) regular modules of a unitary regular Hom-ring:

Theorem 7.9
.

Let
(A,+_{A},\cdot_{A},\alpha)
be a unitary regular Hom-ring with the compatible ring
(A,+_{A}^{\prime},\cdot_{A}^{\prime})
.

1.

If
(M,+_{M},\beta)
is a (left) regular
A
-module then

\beta(a\rhd m)=\alpha(a)\rhd\beta(m),~\;\forall a\in A,~\;m\in M,

(7.12)

where
a\rhd m=\beta^{-1}(am)
defines a
(A,+_{A}^{\prime},\cdot_{A}^{\prime})
-module structure for
(M,\beta^{-1}\circ+_{M})
, it is called the compatible
A
-module of
(M,+_{M},\beta)
.

2.

Suppose that
(M,+_{M}^{\prime})
is a (left) module over
(A,+_{A}^{\prime},\cdot_{A}^{\prime})
for the action
a\rhd m
. If it exists an automorphism of the group
(M,+_{M}^{\prime})
such that (
7.12
) is satisfied, then
(M,+_{M}=\beta\circ+_{M}^{\prime},\beta)
is a (left) module over
(A,+_{A},\cdot_{A},\alpha)
with respect to the action given by
am=\beta(a\rhd m)
, for all
a\in A
and
m\in M
.

Proof
.

1.

(M,+_{M},\beta)
is a (left) regular
A
-module, i.e.,
\beta
is invertible. Then from (
7.6
), we can get easily (
7.12
).

2.

Let
a,~b\in A
and
m_{1},~m_{2}\in M
, from (
7.12
) and the properties of
(M,+_{M}^{\prime})
as a(left)
(A,+_{A}^{\prime},\cdot_{A}^{\prime})
-module, we have

\displaystyle\alpha(a)(m_{1}+_{M}m_{2})=\beta(\alpha(a)\rhd\beta(m_{1}+_{M}^{\prime}m_{2}))

\displaystyle=\alpha^{2}(a)\rhd(\beta^{2}(m_{1})+_{M}^{\prime}\beta^{2}(m_{2}))=\alpha^{2}(a)\rhd\beta^{2}(m_{1})+_{M}^{\prime}\alpha^{2}(a)\rhd\beta^{2}(m_{2})

\displaystyle=\beta(\alpha(a)\rhd\beta(m_{1}))+_{M}^{\prime}\beta(\alpha(a)\rhd\beta(m_{2}))=\alpha(a)\rhd\beta(m_{1}))+_{M}\beta(\alpha(a)\rhd\beta(m_{2})

\displaystyle=\beta(a\rhd m_{1})+_{M}\beta(a\rhd m_{2})

\displaystyle=am_{1}+_{M}am_{2},

(7.13)

\displaystyle(a+_{A}b)\beta(m_{1})=\beta((a+_{A}b)\rhd\beta(m_{1}))=\alpha^{2}((a+_{A}^{\prime}b))\rhd\beta^{2}(m_{1})

\displaystyle=\alpha^{2}(a)\rhd\beta^{2}(m_{1})+_{M}^{\prime}\alpha^{2}(b)\rhd\beta^{2}(m_{1})=\beta(\alpha(a)\rhd\beta(m_{1}))+_{M}^{\prime}\beta(\alpha(b)\rhd\beta(m_{1}))

\displaystyle=\alpha(a)\rhd\beta(m_{1}))+_{M}\beta(\alpha(b)\rhd\beta(m_{1})=\beta(a\rhd m_{1})+_{M}\beta(b\rhd m_{1})

\displaystyle=am_{1}+_{M}bm_{1},

(7.14)

\displaystyle\alpha(a)(bm_{1})=\beta(\alpha(a)\rhd(\alpha(b)\rhd\beta(m)))=\alpha^{2}(a)\rhd\beta(\alpha(b)\rhd\beta(m_{1}))=\alpha^{2}(a)\rhd(\alpha^{2}(b)\rhd\beta^{2}(m_{1}))

\displaystyle=(\alpha^{2}(a)\cdot^{\prime}\alpha^{2}(b))\rhd\beta^{2}(m_{1})=\alpha(a\cdot b)\rhd\beta(m_{1}))=\beta((a\cdot b)\rhd m_{1})

\displaystyle=(a\cdot b)\beta(m),

(7.15)

\displaystyle 1_{A}m=\beta(1_{A}\rhd m_{1})=\alpha(1_{A})\rhd\beta(m_{1})=1_{A}\rhd\beta(m_{1})=\beta(m_{1}).

(7.16)

Therefore
(M,+_{M}=\beta\circ+_{M}^{\prime},\beta)
is a (left) module over
(A,+_{A},\cdot_{A},\alpha)
with respect the action defined by
am=\beta(a\rhd m)
, for all
a\in A
and
m\in M.

7.3
Tensor product of modules

Definition 7.10
.

Let
(R,\alpha_{R})
be a Hom-ring. Let
(A,\alpha_{A})
be a right
R
-module,
(B,\alpha_{B})
a left
R
-module and
(C,\alpha_{C})
be an abelian Hom-group (all abelian Hom-groups having the law of composition denoted by
+
). A map
f:A\times B\rightarrow C
from the Cartesian product of
A
and
B
into
C
is called Hom-R-Bilinear if

1.

f(a_{1}+a_{2},\alpha_{B}(b))=f(a_{1},b)+f(a_{2},b)
,

2.

f(\alpha_{A}(a),b_{1}+b_{2})=f(a,b_{1})+f(a,b_{2})
,

3.

f(\alpha_{A}(a),\alpha_{B}(b))=\alpha_{C}\circ f(a,b)
,

4.

f(ar,\alpha_{B}(b))=f(\alpha_{A}(a),rb)
,

for all
a,~a_{1},~a_{2}\in A,~b,~b_{1},~b_{2}\in B
and
r\in R
. The set of all such Hom-R-bilinear maps from
A\times B
to
C
is denoted by
HL_{R}(A,B;C).

Definition 7.11
.

Let
(R,\alpha_{R})
be a Hom-ring,
(A,\alpha_{A})
be a right regular
R
-module and
(B,\alpha_{B})
be a left regular
R
-module. The tensor product of
A
and
B
, which is denoted by
A\otimes_{R}B
, is a regular abelian Hom-group together with a Hom-R-Bilinear map

\theta:A\times B\rightarrow A\otimes_{R}B,

such that the following universal property holds:
for any Hom-R-bilinear map
f:A\times_{R}B\rightarrow C
, there exists a unique Hom-module homomorphism
\widetilde{f}:A\otimes_{R}B\rightarrow C
such that the diagram:

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern commutes, that is
\widetilde{f}\circ\theta=f.

Proposition 7.12
.

Let
R,~S
be Hom-rings. If
A
is a regular
R-S
-bimodule and
B
is a left regular
S
-module, then
A\otimes_{S}B
is a left
R
-module satisfying

r(a\otimes b)=(ra)\otimes b.

Proof
.

For each
s\in S
, we have

\displaystyle\alpha_{R}(r)(as)\otimes\alpha_{B}(b)

\displaystyle=(ra)\alpha_{S}(s)\otimes\alpha_{B}(b)=\alpha_{A}(ra)\otimes(\alpha_{S}(s)n)=(\alpha_{R}(r)\alpha_{A}(a))\otimes(\alpha_{S}(s)n).

8
Semisimple Modules and Hom-rings

Definition 8.1
.

Let
A
be a Hom-ring and
(M,\beta_{1})
and
(N,\beta_{2})
be two (left)
A
-modules. A homomrophism of Hom-groups
f:N\rightarrow M
is said to be a homomorphism of
A
-modules if
f(n)=\alpha_{A}(a)f(n)
, for all
a\in A
and
n
in
M.

If
f
is the inclusion map, we say that
N
is an
A
-submodule of
M.

Remark 8.2
.

An
A
-submodule of
M
is a Hom-subgroup
N
of
M
which is itself a module under the action of Hom-ring elements.

Note that, for any
A
-module
M
, both
\{0\}
and
M
are trivial
A
-submodules of
M
. Let us now recall that the direct sum of two Hom-groups
(H,\beta_{1})
and
(K,\beta_{2})
(
[
8
]
) is the set

H\oplus K=\{(h,k)|h\in H,~k\in K\},

together with the component-wise operations of the groups
H
and
K
(that is, if
(h_{1},k_{1}),~(h_{2},k_{2})\in H\oplus K
, then
(h_{1},k_{1})(h_{2},k_{2})=(h_{1}h_{2},k_{1}k_{2})
) and the map
\beta_{1}\oplus\beta_{2}
such as
\beta_{1}\oplus\beta_{2}(h,k)=(\beta_{1}(h),\beta_{2}(k)),~\forall(h,k)\in H\oplus K
.
There is a similar structure with modules.

Definition 8.3
.

Let
(M,\beta_{1})
and
(N,\beta_{2})
be modules over a Hom-ring
A
. We define the direct sum of

M
and
N
by

M\bigoplus N=\{m\oplus n|m\in M,~n\in M\}.

(8.1)

The structure of
A
-module of
M\bigoplus N
is given by

(m_{1}\oplus n_{1})\oplus(m_{2}\oplus n_{2})=(m_{1}+_{M}m_{2})\oplus(n_{1}+_{N}n_{2}),

(8.2)

(\beta_{1}\oplus\beta_{2})(m\oplus n)=\beta_{1}(m)\oplus\beta_{2}(n),

(8.3)

a(m\oplus n)=am\oplus an,\;\;\mbox{where}\;\;a\in A.

(8.4)

Definition 8.4
.

Let
A
be a unitary (regular) Hom-ring. An
A
-module
M
is said to be simple if it is non-zero, and has no submodules except
\{0\}
and itself. If
M
is a direct sum of certain simple submodules,
M
is called semisimple.

Lemma 8.5
.

Let
(M,+_{M},\beta)
be a (left) module over a unitary Hom-ring
A
. Then
(ker\beta,+_{M},\beta)
is an
A
-submodule.

Proof
.

For all
m\in Ker\beta
, by
\beta(am)=\alpha(a)\beta(m)=0,
for all
a\in A
, it is easy to show the lemma.

Theorem 8.6
.

Let
(M,+_{M},\beta)
be a (left)simple module over a unitary Hom-ring
A
. Then
M
is regular, i.e., the map
\beta
is invertible.

Proof
.

According to Lemma
8.5
,
(ker\beta,+_{M},\beta)
is an
A
-submodule. Since the module
M
is simple, either
ker\beta=\{0\}
or
ker\beta=M
. The
A
-module
M
is simple, then
ker\beta=\{0\}
. So
\beta
is invertible.

Proposition 8.7
.

Let
(A,+_{A},\cdot_{A},\alpha)
be a unitary regular Hom-ring with the compatible ring
(A,+_{A}^{\prime},\cdot_{A}^{\prime})
. Suppose that
(M,+_{M}^{\prime})
is a simple (left) module over
(A,+_{A}^{\prime},\cdot_{A}^{\prime})
for the action
a\rhd m
. If it exists an automorphism of the group
(M,+_{M}^{\prime})
such that (
7.12
) is satisfied, then
(M,+_{M}=\beta\circ+_{M}^{\prime},\beta)
is a simple (left) module over
(A,+_{A},\cdot_{A},\alpha)
with respect to the action given by
am=\beta(a\rhd m)
, for all
a\in A
and
m\in M
.

Proof
.

Indeed, let
N
be a non-zero
A
-submodule of
(M,+_{M}=\beta\circ+_{M}^{\prime},\beta)
, that is
N\subset M
and
(N,+_{M}=\beta\circ+_{M}^{\prime},\beta)
is an
A
-module with respect to the action defined by
a\otimes n\mapsto an,~\forall a\in A,~n\in N
. According to Theorem
7.9
,
(N,+_{M}^{\prime})
is a module over the ring
(A,+_{A}^{\prime},\cdot_{A}^{\prime})
with respect to the action
a\otimes n\mapsto a\rhd n=\beta^{-1}(an)
, where
\rhd
defines the action of the ring
(A,+_{A}^{\prime},\cdot_{A}^{\prime})
on
(M,+_{M}^{\prime})
. Since
(M,+_{M}^{\prime})
is simple, then
N=M
as groups with respect to the addition
+_{M}^{\prime}
. So from the property
\beta(N)=N
, we can show the result.

The following theorem gives a characterization of (left) modules of a regular unitary Hom-ring.

Theorem 8.8
.

Let
(A,+_{A},\cdot_{A},\alpha)
be a unitary regular Hom-ring with the compatible ring
(A,+_{A}^{\prime},\cdot_{A}^{\prime})
and
(M,+_{M},\beta)
be a simple regular
A
-module. Then the compatible group
(M,+_{M}^{\prime}=\beta^{-1}\circ+_{M})
is a semisimple module over the ring
(A,+_{A}^{\prime},\cdot_{A}^{\prime})
.

Proof
.

Suppose that
(M,+_{M},\beta)
is a regular
A
-module for the action
a\otimes n\mapsto am,~\forall a\in A,~m\in M
, then according to Theorem
7.9
,
(M,+_{M}^{\prime}=\beta^{-1}\circ+_{M})
is a module over
(A,+_{A}^{\prime},\cdot_{A}^{\prime})
for the action
a\otimes n\mapsto a\rhd m=\beta^{-1}(am)
. Let
m
be a non-zero element in
M
and let
k
be the smallest integer such that
\beta^{k-1}(m)\neq m
and
\beta^{k}(m)=m
. The group of
(M,+_{M}^{\prime}=\beta^{-1}\circ+_{M})
,
N=A\rhd m\bigoplus A\rhd\beta(m)\bigoplus\cdot\cdot\cdot\bigoplus A\rhd\beta^{k-1}(m)
is a submodule of
(M,+_{M}^{\prime})
over the ring
(A,+_{A}^{\prime},\cdot_{A}^{\prime})
. Furthermore, we have
\beta(N)=N=Am\bigoplus A\beta(m)\bigoplus\cdot\cdot\cdot\bigoplus A\beta^{k-1}(m)
. Consequently
N
is a submodule of
(M,+_{M},\beta)
over
(A,+_{A},\cdot_{A},\alpha)
. So the simplicity of
(M,+_{M},\beta)
gives
M=N
as a module over
(M,+_{M}^{\prime}=\beta^{-1}\circ+_{M})
. Therefore, as a module over
(A,+_{A}^{\prime},\cdot_{A}^{\prime})
, we have
M=N=A\rhd m\bigoplus A\rhd\beta(m)\bigoplus\cdot\cdot\cdot\bigoplus A\rhd\beta^{k-1}(m),
which shows the semisimplicity of the module
(M,+_{M}^{\prime}=\beta^{-1}\circ+_{M})
over
(A,+_{A}^{\prime},\cdot_{A}^{\prime})
for the action
a\otimes n\mapsto a\rhd m=\beta^{-1}(am)
.

Definition 8.9
.

A Hom-ring
A
is called semisimple (resp. simple) if
A
is semisimple (resp. simple) as an
A
-module.

Theorem 8.10
.

Simples unitary Hom-rings are regular Hom-rings.

Proof
.

The proof is straightforward.

Theorem 8.11
.

Let
(A,+_{A},\cdot_{A},\alpha)
be a unitary Hom-ring with the compatible ring
(A,+_{A}^{\prime},\cdot_{A}^{\prime})
. Then the compatible ring
(A,+_{A}^{\prime},\cdot_{A}^{\prime})
is semisimple.

Proof
.

The proof is straightforward.
